“ IV—Neither Government nor the public adopted any special measures of relief on this occasion. The strong oppressed and looted the weak with impunity.

“ The above is the only information that I have been able to gather. The old records of the family, which were deposited at the Fort of Ankaee, were destroyed on the occasion of the Government taking it. Some papers which were at Vinchoor were destroyed by fire along with the house which was burnt by Holkur.


##### *From Krishnarao Nilkunt Moozumdar.*

“ A letter dated 1st July 1867 from Major Etheridge, Compiler of the Presidency Famine Report, having been received, you requested me in your letter No. 188, dated 22nd idem, to collect and furnish from old Dufturs, family records, or other papers in my possession, or from private sources, such information as might be procurable respecting past famines and scarcities, also the measures of relief which were then adopted, forwarding, at the same time, any documents throwing light on the subject. In reply, I beg to state as follows.

“ 1. The following are the years during which failures of rain and famines occurred :—

“ I—Shuke 1709, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1787. [p. 92] “ II—Shuke 1715, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1793.

“ III—Shuke 1726, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1804—In this year a seer of grain was sold for a Rupee.

“ IV—Shuke 1746, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1824—In this year 2½ pailees of grain were sold for a Rupee.

“ V—Shuke 1754, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1832—In this year want of rain caused the failure of crops, and in some instances the Ryots plundered shops.


##### “ 2. The extent of the distress and its results.

“ I—The disturbances caused by the invasion of Scindia and Holkur, and the hardships which followed thereupon to the Ryots, made them leave the country, and so the soil remained uncultivated. Grain was so scarce that it could not be had for money even by rich people. This occurred in Shuke 1726, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1804.

“ II—Subsequently the Peishwa's Government arranged for supplying the Ryots with grain from other countries, abolished tolls in order to its free importation, and granted remissions of revenue, cowls and advances. By these means people were induced to return and undertake the cultivation of the land.

“ 3. It is not known how far the distress extended. So much is known that it affected the Poona districts.


##### “ 4. The measures adopted for relief were as follow :—

“ I—The Peishwa's Government employed Brahmins to perform propitiatory worship to the god of rain, and distributed charities and alms. These ceremonies were continued even for some years after the introduction of the present rule.

“ II—Whenever want of rain caused distress, the Peishwa's Government granted remissions, advances and cowls, and thus protected the Ryots.

“ I write this from memory and family tradition. No old documents of a nature to furnish information are in my possession.


##### *From Rungrao Venayeh, Poorundhuray.*

“ I received your letter No. 185, dated 22nd July 1867, in which, on a letter dated 1st idem from Major Etheridge, Compiler of the Presidency Famine Report, you desire me to furnish information respecting the times and extents of past famines and scarcities; in reply to which I beg to state as follows :—

“ I—There was a great famine in Shuke 1724, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1802, which is known as the year of disturbance of Holkur. The second famine [p. 93] occcurred in Fuslee 1234, <span class="sc">A. D.</span> 1824, which is called the year of the ‘Khurpud.’ And the third famine has continued from Fuslee 1272, <span class="sc">A. D.</span> 1862, up to the present time, and is styled the current dearness of grain.

“*A.*—The first famine, that of Shuke 1724, <span class="sc">A. D.</span> 1802, resulted from the invasion of Holkur. There was then such a scarcity of grain that it was not procurable for money. Thousands of people died of starvation. The consequence was that the district bore the aspect of a desert.

“*B.*—The second famine, that of Shuke 1746, <span class="sc">A. D.</span> 1824, the year of the ‘Khurpud,’ arose from a general failure of rain. Grain, though old and rotten, was sold at 10 seers a Rupee. Epidemics broke out. There was universal sickness. Some people fled the country. Cattle died from want of grass.

“*C.*—The third famine, the present dearness of grain, is not so severe as those above mentioned. It owes its origin partly to the failure of rain, and partly to the taxes imposed on communities who are barely able to support themselves.

“II—The first famine affected almost the whole of the Deccan. The second, that of Shuke 1746, <span class="sc">A. D.</span> 1824, the districts of Poona, Nuggur, Candeish, and Sholapoor. The third still prevails in all parts of India.

“III—The distress occasioned by the first famine was mitigated by the importation of grain from other countries. Government also reduced the assessment on land, and encouraged and assisted traders to import grain freely.

“IV—The distress caused by the second famine, that of Shuke 1746, <span class="sc">A. D.</span> 1824, was lessened by an opportune fall of rain on the day of ‘Dusera,’ and good crops in some districts. Subsequently harvests became more flourishing.

“V—The distress caused by the third famine is still prevailing. It rains not as required. Servants, labourers, &c., have to pay very high prices for the necessaries of life.

“The above information is stated from what I have heard and gathered from other people. No documentary evidence of a nature to throw light on the subject is with me.


##### *From Munohur Bheorao, Khasnavis and Potnis.*

“In reply to your letters Nos. 183 and 214, dated 24th August 1867, desiring me to collect and furnish from old records, family dufturs and [p. 94] other papers in my possession, or from other sources, information on the subject of the famines and scarcities which have occurred during the late and present Governments, the extent of the distress occasioned, the area of the country affected, and the measures of relief adopted, I beg to state that no documentary evidence of a nature to throw light on this subject is in my possession, but from hearsay and family tradition I am able to say as follows:—

“I—In Shuke 1714, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1792, a famine occurred, during which dearness of grain prevailed to such an extent that a pailee of grain was sold for a Rupee in the Poona districts. In Shuke 1726, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1804, a second famine occurred, during which one seer of grain was sold for a Rupee. These two famines resulted from failure of rain.

“II—The distress extended over the southern part of the Poona districts.

“III—For the relief of the distress the Peishwa’s Government employed Brahmins to perform the usual ceremonies to propitiate the Almighty to send down rain, distributed charities and alms, established free mess-houses, granted advances and cowls, and, after inspection of crops, allowed remission of assessment. Tolls were also abolished in order to the free importation of grain, and traders were encouraged to bring it from other countries. By these means the Ryots were protected.

“IV—After the introduction of the British rule, owing to the failure of rain in Shuke 1746, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1824, grain was sold from 2 to 2½ pailees the Rupee. This distress continued up to Ashween Shood 10th, on which day rain fell and produced good crops. In Shuke 1754, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1832, the grain-sellers raised the price of grain, but Government issued orders and stopped the evil.

“The above is all the information I have been able to collect.


##### *From Nilkuntrao Mahadeo Poorundhuray.*

“In reply to your letter No. 187, dated 2nd July 1867, desiring me to furnish from old dufturs, family records, or other papers in my possession, or from private sources, information respecting past famines and scarcities, together with the measures of relief adopted, forwarding at the same time any documents throwing light on the subject, I regret to state that I am not in a position to comply, having in my possession no documentary evidence, and not deeming tradition or hearsay reliable.


##### *From Kesheorao Ramchundur Jog.*

“In reply to your letter of 22nd July 1867, desiring me to furnish [p. 95] information regarding famines and scarcities prior to and since the introduction of the British rule, I beg to state as follows :—

> " I—During the Peishwa’s rule, in <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1803-4, the armies of Scindia and Holkur came to Poona and fought. Plunder to a great extent was the consequence. There was also a drought, and the crops suffered. Grain was sold from a quarter of a seer to one seer for a Rupee, and even at this rate could be procured with difficulty ; consequently great numbers of people died of starvation. I know nothing regarding the measures adopted for the relief of the distress, as I was very young. I am unable to procure any documents throwing light on the subject, nor can I state precisely the extent of the country affected, nor the period during which the famine lasted.

> " II—After the introduction of the British rule a drought occurred in <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1824, and grain was not sown in sufficient quantities. It consequently became dear. To alleviate the distress Government commenced the construction of the Koorkoomb and Babdeo Ghauts, and thus gave employment to sufferers. The scarcity of grain in those days, however, was not greater than that which has prevailed during the last four years. Fodder was not so dear as it is now. It is said that the scarcity affected an area of about 100 miles round Poona.


#### *From Wamunrao Mahadeo, Raheereckur.*

" I received your letter No. 186, dated 22nd July last, desiring me to furnish information regarding famines and scarcities, and in reply I beg to state as follows :—


##### *During the late Rule.*

> " In Fuslee 1201-2, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1791-92, grain was sold at from 6 to 7 seers for a Rupee in the districts round Poona and in the Concan. Although there was a sufficient fall of rain, and consequent good harvests, still a scarcity arose from the bringing together of the troops intended for the expedition of Khurda. When this was over the country again became prosperous. People did not suffer much from this distress.

> " In Fuslee 1213-14, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1803-4, a Rupee would buy only 1½ or 1¾ seers of grain. This dearness lasted for three or four months. It arose from the incursion of Scindia and Holkur. Throughout the districts of Khandeish, Nuggur and Poona, where it rained, and there were consequent good crops, they were laid waste by the armies of those Chiefs. Other districts were in a flourishing state. When the disturbance ceased the prices of grain began to decline. For the relief of this distress Government granted remissions of revenue, and abolished the customs. Rich people [p. 96] brought grain from other countries, and supplied the poor in charity. Government also employed Brahmins to propitiate the deity to send down rain and bless the sufferers.


##### *During the present Rule.*

> “Fuslee 1233-4, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1823-24, in which year there was a famine, was known as the year of the ‘Khurpud.’ It rained at the commencement of the monsoon, but no rain fell after. On the day of ‘Dusera’ much rain fell, and Ryots who had grain at once sowed it, but no crops resulted. The price of grain rose very high. This distress lasted for the whole year. Government granted Ryots lands in farm, some free from assessment and some on Mamool assessment, and relieved them from distress in this manner.

> “In Fuslee 1242-3, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1832-33, there was a famine, which owed its origin to failure of rain, and high prices resulted.

> “Since Fuslee 1273, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1863, up to the present time the price of grain has continued high. People earn their livelihood with great difficulty. Government partially relieved this distress by granting to their servants compensation on account of high prices, and by sanctioning works of public utility in the Engineering and Railway Departments. Cultivators are enabled to support themselves, and pay Government with ease the light assessment on their fields, from the produce of their crops in good seasons. Moreover, Government have imposed a new tax, and as the poor are unable to work this tax acts as a bar to trade. The Sowkars have become bankrupt. These causes continue to make people pass their days with difficulty.”

46\. Mr. Arthur, the Collector of Sattara, gives very little information, and regrets that “the entire absence of records makes it impossible to supply more.” That gentleman writes on 17th January last as follows:—

> “1. With reference to your Circular of the 1st July last, I have the honor to inform you that no information can be obtained in this district regarding any famines which may have occurred in years beyond the memory of men now living. All the old papers in the possession of families of note in this part of the country were taken away by the Officers of the Inam Commission, and are for the most part, I believe, recorded in your Office.

> “2. The famine years regarding which oral information is forthcoming are <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1792, 1803 and 1824.

> “3. In the first of these years the famine appears to have raged throughout the Sattara territory. It was occasioned partly by the scanty [p. 97] fall of rain, and partly by the political troubles which then prevailed. It lasted, it seems, about a year, during which time grain was sold for the most part at 3 seers per Rupee.

“4. The Peishwa’s Government is said to have then endeavoured, as far as possible, to relieve the distress which was so generally felt throughout the Deccan, but I cannot find that any public works of importance in the Sattara territory had their origin in his liberality.

“5. The dearth of <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1803 does not appear to have had its origin in a failure of crops in this part of the country: in the Nizam’s country, and also in Goozerat, there appears to have been a great failure of crops, owing to which people flocked in great numbers from distant parts to Sattara, and the result was to create a famine similar to that which prevailed elsewhere. This famine is said to have reached its height in the months of July and August, and stories up to this time are told, with horror, of men having been reduced to the extremity of subsisting on human flesh. Corn is said to have been sold at one seer per Rupee, and so many as 25,000 strangers are said to have flocked into the town of Waee to obtain what they could from the liberality of the Punt Pritinidhi, the Rastia, and such like wealthy persons. It is stated that no fewer than 10,000 persons died in Waee during this famine.

“6. The dearth of <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1824 does not appear to have been so severe, and perhaps was more local in its nature. Grain was then sold, it is said, at about 6 seers for a Rupee, and people emigrated for food both in the direction of Nuggur and of Kolapoor.

“7. I regret that I can only give this general account regarding what has occurred in this district, but the entire absence of records makes it impossible to supply more detailed information.”

47\. From Sholapoor the Sub-Collector, Mr. G. W. Anderson, reported on 6th September that there were no official records which gave any account of past famines, and that he could do no more than forward what information the Mumludars had been able to obtain from private sources. The several Mumludars and the District Deputy Collector wrote as follows:—


##### *Sholapoor Mamludar.*

“ Since the introduction of the British rule, in Fuslee 1227, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1817, a great famine, called ‘Kurdee famine,’ occurred in Fuslee year 1228, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1818, when people lived by eating ‘Kurdee.’ Two famines afterwards raged in Fuslee years 1241-42 and 1264-65, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1831-32 and 1854-55, owing to want of rain, and they extended over the whole of the Talooka. [p. 98] It does not appear that Government afforded any other means of relief to the famine-stricken people except by employing them on works of public utility. The merchants also lent their aid to the distressed by distributing grain and food to them. Before the commencement of the British rule the rate of grain was 1¼ seer for the Rupee, and the Peishwa's Government distributed food to the sufferers.


##### *Barsee Mamludar.*

> “The great Doorga Deevee famine took place in <span class="sc">A. D.</span> 1396, and spread over the whole of Hindostan. Rain fell slightly in <span class="sc">A. D.</span> 1794, when there was a war carried on between the Peishwa and the Nizam. Much grain was then plundered, and the standing crops were destroyed by armies, &c., traversing the country. Grain was then selling at 1½ seers for the Rupee. The Peishwa's Government did not give any relief to the sufferers. In Fuslee year 1213, <span class="sc">A. D.</span> 1803, the rate of grain was still higher. It was sold at 1¼ seer for the Rupee, owing to no rain having fallen. The Peishwa and the Nimbalkur protected the people from the scourge of famine by giving them food and clothes. In <span class="sc">A. D.</span> 1824-25, too, there was a famine owing to the scarcity of rain. There was a failure of crops. Ten bundles of Kurbee were sold for the Rupee. Many cattle died for the want of Kurbee. This famine extended over the whole of the Talooka. Government were pleased to remit assessment on the lands in consequence. In <span class="sc">A. D.</span> 1832-33 and 1845-46, owing to rain not having fallen seasonably, grain was sold at 8 seers for the Rupee. Government were pleased in these years also to make remissions of assessment.


##### *Marah Mamludar.*

> “In Fuslee year 1202-3, <span class="sc">A. D.</span> 1792-3, the rate of grain was 5 seers for the Rupee, owing to the marauding incursions of Scindia and Holkur. No information is forthcoming as to the measures, if any, adopted for the relief of the poor. The famine extended over 200 ‘koss.’ The rate of grain in Fuslee year 1213, <span class="sc">A. D.</span> 1803, was 1¼ seer for the Rupee, owing partly to its having been plundered by rebels, and partly to its scarcity. Many persons died of starvation. In <span class="sc">A. D.</span> 1824-25, 1832-33, 1845-46 and 1854-55, arose a general outcry of distress from want of rain, when, by way of relief, some works were carried on.


##### *Kurmalla Mamludar.*

> “In Fuslee year 1204, <span class="sc">A. D.</span> 1794, in consequence of no rain having fallen, and because of the war between the Nizam and the Peishwa, grain was sold at 5 seers for the Rupee.


<!-- page: 111 | folio: 99 -->
“In Fuslee year 1214, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1804, because of the absence of rain and of the devastation caused by the forces of Scindia and the Pindarees, grain was sold at 1¾ or 2 seers for the Rupee. Many persons died of starvation. The Nimbalkur afforded relief to the sufferers by distributing food.

“In <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1824-25 there was a great scarcity of grain: it was sold at 10 seers for the Rupee. The scarcity extended over about 200 miles.


##### *The District Deputy Collector.*

“1. I have the honor to acknowledge the receipt of your endorsement No. 1440, of the 12th July last, forwarding copy of a letter from Major Etheridge, and requesting to be furnished with information regarding past famines in the Talookas in my charge.

“2. Immediately on the receipt of your orders, I addressed the Mumladars, and asked them to use every endeavour to obtain full information from every available source, and especially to take copies of all documentary evidence bearing on the subject.

> _[Margin: <sup>*</sup> Of Punderpoor and Sangola.]_

“3. From the report of Mumladars,<sup>*</sup> which I enclose, it will be noticed that they have not been able to obtain any documents, owing to the Officers. of the Inam Commission having removed all old records from the districts. The information embodied in the reports has been obtained by oral enquiries among old inhabitants. The reports give full details. I shall annex a succinct account of the more important features.

“4. The oldest famine about which information can be obtained is the one that is said to have occurred about <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1396, and to have lasted 12 years. It is known by the name of the ‘Doorga Devee’ famine. It extended over the whole of India south of the Nurbudda, and depopulated whole districts. It arose from the total want of seasonable rain. It cannot be ascertained whether any measures of relief were adopted at the time.

“5. The next in order is the famine said to have occurred about the year <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1460, and the memory of which has been perpetuated by a poetical legend very popular in the Deccan, and known as ‘Damajee Punt’s Akhyan’ (history). Damajee, the legend states, was a Brahmin, and employed at Mungul Vedh, about 12 miles from Punderpoor, as an Officer of revenue under the Mogul Government. He had collected a large quantity of corn at Mungul Vedh on account of the Government. Owing to a famine which broke out, hundreds of Brahmins and others flocked to Mungul Vedh, and were fed by Damajee out of the Government stores. The Mogul Emperor was wrath, and issued orders that Damajee should be arrested and carried to his presence to receive merited punishment. While Damajee was being thus led to suffer for doing an act of justifiable benevolence, [p. 100] Vittoba, the great Idol of Punderpoor, and the patron divinity of Damajee, took compassion upon his devoted servant, and, assuming the garb of a village Mhar, went to the great Emperor's court, and paid in cash for the whole of the corn distributed by Damajee. Such is an outline of this legend, which possesses some merit as a poetical composition, and is the production of a mind impressed by a deep feeling of devotion. At Mungul Vedh the village priests point out the alleged site of Damajee's house and of the corn-cellars. This famine, which also was the result of want of rain, lasted for a year, and is said to have been felt in the Deccan and Southern India.

“6. A famine of equal extent and duration to that described in the last paragraph is said to have occurred about the year 1520. This is said to have been caused by successions of Military hordes destroying the crops and plundering every thing that came in their way. No measures of relief were adopted.

“7. The price at which grain used to sell during the famines mentioned above has not been ascertained.

“8. A. D. 1791 was another year of famine. The distress in the Carnatic was excessive, as the crops there had entirely failed. In the Deccan about half, or a fourth, of the usual yield was obtained, but in consequence of thousands having come from the Carnatic to the Deccan for support, the distress throughout was great. Thefts became the order of the day, and large numbers fell victims to the scourge. The rigor of the calamity lasted about four months. Nothing appears to have been done to relieve the distress. Corn sold at 3 seers per Rupee.

“9. In A. D. 1802 the country was the scene of perpetual raids and plunder. Scindia's armies were in the field. Hordes of freebooters also carried fire and sword everywhere. The crops were destroyed, and large numbers of people were killed. The consequence of this state of things was naturally a famine, which extended over the Deccan, Candeish and Southern India. A large number of persons is said to .have died for want of food. Dhondbhut Kutkey, an inhabitant of Punderpoor, is said to have fed many at that town; and an officer of the Nimbulkur's, stationed at Mohole, is also reputed to have given food to many for several days. There are no means of ascertaining the extent of these private charities. The intensity of the famine was felt during three months, and at the height of the visitation corn sold as dear as ¾ seer per Rupee.

“10. There was a partial famine in the districts of Punderpoor, Barsee, Tooljapoor and the surrounding country, in 1818, partly on account of the ravages made by Bajeerow's armies, and partly by the total failure of crops towards Baleghat. A Sowcar of Vyrog in the Barsee Talooka, [p. 101] surnamed Ghongday, is said to have given food to all who resorted to him for it. How many persons were thus succoured by him cannot be ascertained. Cholera of a fearful type also broke out this year, sending thousands to an untimely grave. It may be remarked *en passant* that this was the first recognized appearance of Cholera on this side of India. Grain sold at 3 to 4 seers per Rupee during the height of the famine.

> “11. In 1824 was also experienced a partial famine in the Punderpoor and surrounding districts from the Bheema to the Godavery. This was caused by want of seasonable rain. Scindia had kept an ‘Anchutra’ at Punderpoor, and the Ghongdays of Vyrog had their Chutra also, but for the relief of the large majority of sufferers no organized arrangements were made. This scarcity lasted for a year. Corn sold at 6 to 8 seers a Rupee.

> “12. The last famine remaining to be noticed is that which occurred in 1832. This was also caused by want of seasonable rain, and prevailed in the Sholapoor and adjoining provinces, embracing an area of nearly 150 square miles. Many left their lands and homes. Government had to grant remissions. Maharoodra Bapoo Kulowdeb, a Brahmin Sowcar, and many Marwaree Sowcars, are said to have fed thousands for several weeks at Sholapoor. The Ghongdays of Vyrog acted with their usual generosity. Public works were also commenced by Government to provide work for the helpless. The famine lasted nine or ten months, and corn used to sell at 5 to 6 seers a Rupee.”

48\. The Political Agent at Kolapoor, Lieutenant Colonel G. S. Anderson, also reported on 26th November that no records of famines in the Kolapoor districts prior to the present century were forthcoming; and added that—

> “3. These districts being situated near the Ghauts, and having generally a good rain-fall, do not appear within the present century to have suffered from any severe famine caused by a local failure of crops. Occasionally the seasons have been bad, and prices have been unusually high, but there has been no year which could be styled a famine year, simply as regards the tract of country embraced in the Kolapoor territory. In 1804-5, however, although the season was a tolerably good one in Kolapoor itself, all the effects of a famine were experienced from the large number of starving emigrants coming from other districts. The causes of the scarcity in more remote districts can best be gleaned from information obtained from the British Collectorates. The main cause assigned by the Kolapoor Officials is the ruthless course of devastation to which the Mahratta armies subjected the country in the interior. Holkur’s wanton destruction of the crops and grain supplies of the Godavery districts is especially [p. 102] referred to. Kolapoor then became a refuge for many destitute persons, and the consequent pressure on the local supplies caused the price of grain to rise to 2 or 1<span class="sc">½</span> seers of different kinds of grain per Rupee. A good many deaths from starvation appeared to have occurred, but there is no reliable information from which even an approximate estimate of their number could be given. The ruling Raja of Kolapoor, Sivajee, by all accounts, did his best to mitigate the calamity by the distribution of boiled food to the starving people who had flocked to Kolapoor from other quarters. The Himmut Bahader, the Bheem Bahader, and other Sirdars, and well-to-do persons, are said to have followed the good example set by the Raja. Luckily the following year was one of great abundance, which enabled the effects of this severe distress to be quickly recovered from.

“4. As above mentioned, there have been other years of scarcity and high prices, but they do not appear to require special mention as famine years.

“5. I regret the meagreness of this statement, but there is always a difficulty in getting full or reliable information from the records of these Native States. Moreover, Kolapoor, from its situation, has happily been more exempt from famine than most Indian districts, and thus does not afford much matter for report.”

49\. The Collector of Belgaum, Mr. Grey, on 18th February last stated that the information forthcoming regarding past famines in that district also was “extremely scanty,” that his endeavours had entirely failed to bring forth any thing of any value, and that he could do nothing more than quote oral tradition. Mr. Grey wrote as follows:—

“2. The information forthcoming regarding past famines in this district is extremely scanty. Previous to the introduction of the British rule no record of such occurrences appears to have been kept, and references to the Mumladars to procure such Native records and papers that may be in the possession of the hereditary district and village officers have entirely failed to bring forth any thing of any value. Such information as is procurable rests entirely on oral tradition, and from this it may be gathered that throughout the Southern Mahratta Country, in consequence of excessive drought and swarms of locusts, there was a gradual failure of crops, which commenced from Fuslee year 1197, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1787-88, and continued to <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1795-96, and caused great distress among all classes. The famine appears to have been at its height about 1791-92, and it having extended to the country bordering on the Godavery the people from those parts flocked in numbers into this province, thereby raising the price of food to such an [p. 103] extent that it was scarcely procurable. It is said that some of the higher classes being unable to obtain grain at any price, and rejecting animal food, poisoned themselves, while the poorer classes found a scanty subsistence from roots, herbs, dead animals, and even human corpses. Altogether 25,000 persons are said to have died from starvation in the town and district of Gokak alone. There is a tradition that a woman in Gokak was driven by hunger to devour her own offspring, but that the indignation of the late Government was so great that she was immediately put to death by being tied to the feet of a buffaloe, and thus trodden to death.

“3. It does not appear that any specific measures of relief were set on foot by the late Government. Private charity was exercised by the Jagheerdars to the extent of their ability, but the country being entirely land-locked there was no communication with other districts, and after the supplies in store were exhausted grain was not obtainable by any classes.

“4. The next famine appears to have occurred in the years Fuslee 1212 to 1214, <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1802-03 to 1804-5, and was caused by excessive drought by immigration of people from the Godavery districts and the march of the Peishwa's army through the country, which in many parts quite put a stop to cultivation. The people suffered nearly as much from distress in these years as they did from the famine which prevailed in 1787-88, and no organized measures of relief appear to have been set on foot by the late Government. It is said that 15,000 persons died in the Gokak district alone, but there is no record whatever obtainable regarding this famine.

“5. There appears to have been great scarcity in the Southern Mahratta Country in the year 1832, and prices were very high, but the distress cannot be said to have amounted to a famine; and although some people are said to have sold their children to obtain food, there is no reliable information obtainable that any deaths occurred. Belgaum was not formed into a separate Collectorate till the year 1836, so the records of this year are all with the Collector of Dharwar, and I have not had an opportunity of referring to them.

“6. I regret that the information forthcoming on this subject is so meagre; but if you can point out any sources in this district from which you consider that any reliable history can be derived, I shall be happy to make further enquiries.”

50\. Under these circumstances it would simply have been waste of time to suggest that further minute enquiries should be made, for eight months had already been occupied in procuring the above meagre account.


<!-- page: 116 | folio: 104 -->
51\. Nor was more ample information forthcoming from the Kulladghee Collectorate. Mr. Armstrong in like manner, after eight months' search and labour, reported as follows on 21st February last.

> "1. With reference to your Circular of 1st July last, I have the honor to report that the famines that occurred in the Talookas now forming the Kulladghee district are as follows :—


##### *Before the introduction of the British Rule.*

> "2. In Fuslee 1201, A. D. 1791, there was a famine owing to want of rain, and no stores of grain to fall back on. No measures of relief were taken, and the people appear to have been much harassed by the marching of troops. There were very many deaths from want of food. This year was called 'Dogee Burra,' two Canarese words, 'Dogee,' skull, and 'Burra,' a famine. The interpretation given to me is that people died in such number from hunger that they could not be buried.

> "3. In Fuslee 1213, A. D. 1803, the rain-fall was good, and so were the crops, but owing to the Pindarees there was a famine. Grain sold from 2 to 4 Bengal seers the Rupee, and in the Beejapoor Talooka as high as 1½ seers the Rupee. No measures for relief were taken, but in the Bagulcote 'Prant' some of the merchants fed the starving. The distress appears to have been great, and people died from want. This year was called 'Ragee Burra' famine, in which people ate 'Ragee.'

> "4. As regards these two famines no documents are procurable from the Zemindars, or other hereditary officers. The information obtained has been from what people have heard one from the other.


##### *British Rule.*

> "5. In 1818—20 there was a famine from want of rain in the Talookas of Moodebehal, Hypurga, Indee, Beejapoor, and Mungolee, which lasted from six to nine months. In the Indee Talooka there was something of a crop, but it was soon eaten up, as people in quest of food crowded in from the Nizam's territories. 'Jowaree' was sold at from 6 to 8 Bengal seers a Rupee. A considerable number of people and cattle are said to have died from starvation. People were reduced to eating tamarind seeds parched in the fire. There is nothing to show that any measures for relief were taken to alleviate the distress.

> "6. In 1824-25 in the Talookas of Indee, Moodebehal, Mungolee and Hypurga, there was a famine owing to want of rain. 'Jowaree' sold at 16 Bengal seers the Rupee. No deaths from want appear to have taken place. There is nothing to show that any measures were taken to relieve the distress.


<!-- page: 117 | folio: 105 -->
"7. In 1832-33 there was a failure of crop for want of rain throughout the whole Talookas of the district, *viz.*, Indee, Hypurga, Moodebehal, Mungolee, Beejapoor, Bagulcote, Hoongoond, and Badamee. 'Jowaree' sold at 8 Bengal seers the Rupee. Mr. Arbuthnot, the Sub-Collector at Bagulcote, distributed food to the people gratis in the Indee and Moodebehal, Mungolee and Hypurga Talookas, and also employed the people in making roads. Many deaths are said to have been caused by starvation, and the mortality among cattle to have been great.

"8. In 1853-54 there was a crop throughout the district, with the exception of the Indee Talooka, where it failed. Owing to a failure of crop in the Sholapoor State, the people emigrated from there. Prices rose, and 'Jowaree' sold in the Indee Talooka at 7 Bengal seers the Rupee. In the other Talookas it sold at 25 seers the Rupee. No deaths are reported to have occurred from want. Owing to the high prices of grain the people were employed in making roads and on other works in the Talookas of Indee and Hypurga.

"9. From 1863-64 to 1866-67 the crops have not been good from want of rain, but owing to the people being well off, and to large supplies of grain from Sholapoor, distress was but little felt. In 1865-66 the sum of Rs. 10,000 was sanctioned for works on which to employ the people."

52\. From Dharwar Mr. Reid, the Collector, reported on 19th February that he had not been able to find out any other particulars of past famines than those recorded in the following statements by his subordinates :—


##### *By Mr. King, First Assistant Collector.*

"The information which I have been able to obtain is very meagre, and regarding the earlier famines the records are silent. The most destructive famine is the earliest of which I have any account, and appears to have taken place in 1791—93, but I cannot give the date with accuracy. It is known by the picturesque name of the 'Dogi Bura,' or Golgotha famine. It is still remembered in the districts of Hooblee, Dumbul and Kulghutgee. The people were reduced to feeding on leaves and berries, and women and children were sold. From Dumbul I am informed that the rain-fall failed for twelve years, and that for three there was no cultivation. The Mysore Government, to which these districts were subject, appears to have seized the stores of grain in Hooblee, and the rich are said to have come forward to the assistance of their poorer brethren. One seer of grain of only half the capacity of the present seer[^sec-03-fn-003] sold for 10 annas and 8 pies in Dumbul. The next famine of note appears to have taken place about sixty-three years ago, but I cannot furnish [p. 106] the date exactly. The Deshpandey of Seegaum remembers it, and he tells me that it lasted for a year, and was caused by a great immigration of starving people from the country about Punderpoor and Bejapoor. It is still known in the Hooblee district as the ‘Bayan Bura,’ or ‘Terrible’ famine. Grain seems to have been sold at 5 or 6 seers a Rupee, and the wealthy supplemented the wants of the poorer classes, being headed in Hooblee by Chintamonrao Putwurdhun. It appears to have extended through all my districts, and I cannot find that any steps were taken for its amelioration by the Native Government. In 1832, in consequence of a failure of rain and an immigration from districts beyond the Krishna, great scarcity was experienced in all the districts. The price of grain varied from 7 seers in Hooblee to 9 in Dumbul, but in the latter Talooka some of the poorest class were reduced to eating grass. The rich in Hooblee, headed by Appoorao Lokhundy, subscribed for the relief of the poor, and Government remitted the tolls. Mr. Elliot, the Collector, also issued an order forbidding forestalling and regrating, and requiring the dealers to bring their grain into the market. Tanks and other works of utility were undertaken to provide labour for the poor. The three famines noticed above appear to have been general. From Dumbul I hear of two minor famines; one in 1814 was called the ‘Bisaghee Bura,’ a name the meaning of which I have not been able to discover. It was apparently caused by cultivation being stopped owing to the disturbed state of the country. It only lasted two months, during which grain was sold at 7 seers the Rupee. I imagine that it cannot have been confined to Dumbul only, but its limits are not given. In 1824 another short famine is recorded, also lasting two months, in which grain was sold at 12 seers the Rupee. It is attributed to a failure in the rain supply, and appears to have extended into the Belgaum districts. I have not sketched the famine of last year, as the reports made by the Collector to Government contain all the information regarding it required by Major Etheridge.


#### *By the Deputy Collector.*

“ 1. With reference to your endorsement No. 1253, dated 10th July last, calling for information as to past famines in this district, I beg to report that, though considerable search has been made among the old records, I have been unable to discover any information on the subject.

“ 2. I beg, however, to append a memorandum forwarded to me by Ramrow Bhimajee, late Akburnavees of Kolapoor, descriptive of a famine which occurred in the districts lying east of Dharwar about sixty-six or sixty-seven years ago. A translation of the memorandum is annexed.

“ 3. As a report on the famine which appeared in the Dharwar districts in 1866 has been already laid by you before Government, it will, I presume, be unnecessary for me to make any report thereon.


<!-- page: 119 | folio: 107 -->

##### *By Ramrao Bhimajee, late Akburnavees at Kolapoor.*

> " 1. With reference to your order for information regarding past famines in the Carnatic, I beg to report that about sixty-six or sixty-seven years ago, owing to drought and failure of crops, the inhabitants of Dousall in the Beejapoor districts and the surrounding villages swarmed into this part of the country—I mean Dharwar, &c. On their way they could get neither food to eat nor water to drink. They arrived at Dharwar in a most deplorable condition, and, finding no shelter and food here, laid themselves down amongst the bushes of Shegeekaee (a prickly plant, which then grew around the fort), and numbers of them died for want of food.

> " 2. At that time this country was blessed with good harvest seasons and rain. Information of the expected arrival of foreigners reached the community of Dharwar about two years before. At this time the insurrection headed by Donjibag occurred, and provisions became scarce. Those who had grain to spare did not venture to assist the sufferers, under the impression that they would have to share the same fate in case of the famine extending to Dharwar.

> " 3. How far the famine had spread in the Beejapoor district could not be traced, but this district was free from it.

> " 4. No measures were taken by the Government of the day to relieve the sufferers, nor was any assistance afforded to such of them as had repaired to this part in hope of getting food. It was then the Peishwa’s Government.

> " 5. Of the above no documentary evidence is forthcoming, but it can be traced that this famine was called ‘ Dogi Bara,’ as the sufferers died striking their heads against each other. ‘ Dogi’ is a Canarese word signifying skull. Another reason for styling it ‘ Dogi Bura’ is that most of the inhabitants above mentioned died on the way. Thousands of dead bodies were to be seen lying on the highways. The bodies, after lying for some time, were removed and buried by ‘ Mahars.’ This famine is also called ‘ Byan Bura,’ because those who came to this part of the country begged food on the way-side villages in a piteous tone, and when they got no assistance they laid down and died.’

53\. Mr. Reid’s report on the famine of 1866 is given in full below :—

> " 1. The distress in the Dharwar districts reached its height in the months of August, September and October 1866. The Talookas in which the greatest destitution appeared were Nuwulgoond, Roan and Dumbul, eastward of the station of Dharwar.


<!-- page: 120 | folio: 108 -->
> "2. The distress may be stated as the result of several successive bad seasons. Though the monsoon of 1866 in Dharwar set in late, a fair harvest was looked for until August, when, owing to the rains keeping off, grain had not only become dear, but scarce in the market, and most distressing accounts of the condition into which the poorer classes were plunged continued to reach the Collector throughout August and September. Very little rain had fallen throughout the districts up to the end of September, when heavy and continued falls saved the crops.

> "3. With a view to afford relief to the sufferers, and to provide work for the unemployed, beneficial public works, for which skilled labour was not necessary, were commenced in the Talookas of Dharwar, Dumbul, Roan and Nuwulgoond. A special grant of 40,000 Rupees was made by Government from Local Funds, and Rupees 3,500 from Imperial Funds for the purpose. A special grant of 50,000 Rupees was also given for the improvement of the high road from Teygoor *via* Dharwar to Hurreeher, and an advance of Rs. 16,000 promised, if necessary, to the Municipality of Dharwar for the improvement of a large tank in the suburbs of the town.

> "4. Considerable numbers of the poor thus found employment for several months until the harvest was gathered, which the seasonable late rains of September and October saved.

> "5. The following extracts from reports made to the Collector in September by the First Assistant Collector and District Deputy Collector respectively of this district show the extent to which the distress prevailed.


##### *Extract paragraphs* 3, 4 *and* 5, *of Mr. Hope's report No.* 45, *dated* 15*th September* 1866.

> "" 3. The distress existing in the Dumbul Talooka is the result not of one year's bad harvest, but of a continual failure of crops for some three or four years past. Even now, however, it is owing (at least at Gudug) not so much to actual scarcity of food as to the dearness of food in consequence of apprehensions as to the approaching harvest. The Ryots who have grain stored are naturally reluctant to part with it lest they should afterwards come to stand in need of it. Rain has, up to the present date, been very deficient in this Talooka. There was a little a month or so ago, barely sufficient to enable the cultivators to sow some of their fields, but a large portion of the country is lying waste, and most of the crops in those fields which have been sown are being blighted for want of moisture. A sufficient [p. 109] supply of rain is by no means yet despaired of (it is known to have fallen lately in the surrounding country); and if it comes it cannot but have a most beneficial effect upon the grain market.

> “ ‘4. The gloomy prospects of the season are felt greatly by the people. The cultivators are prevented from following their usual pursuits, many people have been reduced to beggary, and still more have left their homes and gone to seek a livehood elsewhere. The town of Dambal has been deserted by its inhabitants to a great extent, and presents a very melancholy appearance. Many of the deserted homes have been left carelessly open, but most of them have been shut up, indicating an intention on the part of the people to return to them when better times come.

> “ ‘5. Both in Gudug and in Dambal steps have been taken for the feeding of those who are utterly destitute and incapacitated from work. This is, of course, a much larger class at Gudug (including Bettigherry) than at Dambal. Subscriptions have been raised at both places sufficient to preserve this class from starvation for some time to come. The emergency has called forth highly commendable munificence on the part of some members of the wealthier Native community, especially at Gudug, where one individual (Muddiwallupa Bullee) has, for two months, fed about 1,000 people daily at his own expense.’


##### *Extract paragraphs 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5, of Mr. Hearn's report No. 33, dated 11th September 1866, to the address of the Collector of Dharwar.*

> “ ‘1. With reference to your endorsement No. 1158, dated 13th ultimo, I have the honor to submit my opinion and report as to the best works to be undertaken in the Nuwulgoond and Roan Talookas for the relief of the sufferers among the poorer classes from the present scarcity of grain and other causes.

> “ ‘2. You are probably aware that there has been a large influx of people into these and other Talookas of the Dharwar Collectorate from the neighbouring Collectorates of Bellary, Kulladghee, and Belgaum. The people from Bellary have left it principally on account of the famine prices prevailing there: those from Kulladgee and Belgaum have been driven here, as I am informed, by the high prices of grain ruling there, as well as the ravages of cholera (which has since abated) in some of the villages bordering on the Dharwar Collectorate.

> “ ‘3. During my journey from Dharwar to Nuwulgoond on the 3rd instant I met a great number of people (inhabitants of this and the surrounding districts) proceeding towards Dharwar, who, with scarcely any exception, stated they were driven to do so to seek employment as they were suffering from want, and I need hardly [p. 110] observe that their wretched appearance fully bore out what they said. In one or two instances the parties were so weak, evidently from hunger, that they were obliged to lie down to rest themselves. From the enquiries which I have verbally made I have reason to believe that large numbers of our villages have, from their impoverished circumstances, been obliged to content themselves with one scanty meal a day.

“ ‘4. All the tanks and some of the wells which I inspected since leaving Dharwar I found completely dry; the villagers being thus obliged to resort to Nullahs and holes several miles away, the supply of water in which being small occupied a great deal of time in collection and conveyance. It will thus be seen that the poor are suffering from the want of the very necessaries of life, viz., food and water.

“ ‘5. On the last market days at Nuwulgoond and Nurgoond grain was sold at the following rates :—


| Col 1 | Col 2 | Col 3 |
| --- | --- | --- |
| “ ‘ Nuwulgoond | Rice (coarse) at 3 seers per Rupee. | Jowaree at 3½ per Rupee. |
| “ ‘ Nurgoond | Rice (coarse) at 3 seers per Rupee. | Jowaree at 3½ per Rupee.’ |

“ 6. The following memorandum recorded in 1866 by the then Acting llector, Mr. Shaw Stewart, details so fully the arrangements made for eviating the distress that prevailed that the Collector begs to be excused quoting it at length.


##### *Dharwar Collector’s Office, 20th September* 1866.

“ ‘ 1. It is necessary to lay down some instructions for the guidance of District Officers if the present scarcity and distress lasts longer. It is evident that unless rain falls generally within ten days throughout those portions of the Collectorate where dry crops are grown the coming harvest will be a failure ; and even if rain falls there is no doubt, from the reports I have received, that the chance of a fair crop is in many places already gone, while every day’s delay in the fall of rain adds to the extent of failure.

“ ‘ 2. This is especially to be regretted because of the small quantity of grain that is now in store in the district, in consequence of the demands made on the grain reserves during the last three seasons of deficient harvests; and it seems to be generally considered that even if the grain in stores were made available there would not be enough to supply food for the population till the harvest of 1867-68.


<!-- page: 123 | folio: 111 -->
> “ ‘ 3. There is thus every prospect of the present high prices continuing unabated for a lengthened period, and they may even become still more exorbitant. Distress and suffering will thus become greater.

> “ ‘ 4. Under these circumstances, the first thing that the Officers of Government have to do is to endeavour to find employment for the unemployed poor, and the second thing is to provide against the contingency of the supplies of food becoming exhausted.

> “ ‘ 5. As regards employment, the Acting Executive Engineer and I have, by the permission of the Revenue Commissioner, directed the commencement of various large public works in the Talookas where the distress has been greatest, on which works it is calculated that about Rs. 2,000 will be spent every day till the end of this month, and this will undoubtedly relieve many from pressure of want and famine.

> “ ‘ 6. But it is necessary to look forward to the future, and to be prepared to find employment for the poor so long as the present high prices prevail.

> “ ‘ 7. The kinds of works that are most desirable to undertake for this purpose are those that give employment to the greatest number of persons, men, women and children, where no great professional skill is required, and which have for their object the improvement of water supply, or traffic communications. No work is so useful and appropriate as excavating and removing earth from large tanks or preparing metal for roads; but there are others that can be advantageously carried on, such as road-making, digging holes on the road sides for trees, or digging wells.

> “ ‘ 8. I wish all the Mamludars, Mahalkurees and Foujdars, to look at the matter with a view to the future, and be prepared whenever there are many unemployed poor persons in any place to propose some useful work on which to employ them. I will approve and sanction any such work being commenced by the Mamludars whenever the emergency is such that neither my own nor the Assistant Collector’s orders can be waited for. This may happen in the case of a gang of poor people coming unexpectedly from other districts, but ought not to be necessary if precaution is used. Generally speaking, the orders of the Collector, or Assistant Collector, should be asked for before any new work is commenced, and in the event of any such application being received by an Assistant Collector he will exercise his discretion in ordering its commencement at once, or refer to me for orders. In the event of his ordering such a work to be begun at once, the Mamludar is desired and empowered to make a daily payment of wages out of the [p. 112] Treasury till such time as the Hoozoor orders are received. All such proceedings should be fully reported to the Hoozoor without loss of time.

> “ ‘9. It is very important that all wandering gangs should be stopped and employed on work. I beg that this may be carefully attended to, and that no person be allowed to wander about the country in search of food, or alms, or work.

> “ ‘10. The Shetsundees are reported to suffer much from distress at this time: for this purpose such of them as can be spared from the necessary duties in their villages ought to be employed in connection with these works as overseers of gangs, or to guard tools, or stores, or treasure, and receive proper wages for this duty, independently of their official emoluments.

> “ ‘11. Where large bodies of labourers are collected at any work great care should be used to see that the ground near them is kept clean, and that they get proper water and food. All precautions must be taken to prevent disease breaking out among them, and on these duties the Shetsundees might be employed.

> “ ‘12. A small percentage of the outlay, not exceeding two per cent., may be spent on extra establishment for supervision and accounts.

> “ ‘13. With regard to preparing metal, I mention the following roads on the sides of which large heaps of metal may be collected—

- 1. Teygoor to Hurreeher throughout.
- 2. Hooblee to Koonoor, at Hooblee and Nurgoond, and any other places where stone is procurable.
- 3. Hooblee to Gudug, at Koosoogul, Annigherry and Gudug, and any other places where stone is procurable.
- 4. Dharwar to Hullial.
- 5. Hooblee to Sungtteecope.
- 6. Bunkapoor to Moondgode.
> Detailed instructions will be issued by the Executive Engineer and by me for this work; in the mean time I only mention the places and roads where large heaps of metal may be formed.

> “ ‘14. For these works I think that for the present 3 annas should be the highest rate of wages for an ordinary labourer, 2½ for a woman, and 2 or 1½ for a child; and I should prefer that the day’s wages should be paid in cash at the end of each day, as the trouble of mustering will be avoided. It is not necessary to pay each person [p. 113] separately our supply of copper would run short, but a family or party might be paid in silver through the head-man. I shall, however, be glad to receive any suggestions for altering these rules of pay and the method of payment. Perhaps in some cases grain payments may be necessary, but I think that it is better, if possible, to provide a few Bunyas' shops near each large work, and to let the people buy food with their wages, due encouragement and protection being given to the Bunyas.

> “ ‘15. The second matter referred to above will, perhaps, prove to be more important than the supply of work, namely, the supply of food, and I beg all the Mamludars will turn their attention to the subject, and consider whether there is enough grain in the district to supply the population with food if this harvest should fail partially or wholly; and if there is not enough that they will consider and ascertain whether it can be procured from the neighbouring districts, or whether supplies are likely to flow in by the ordinary action of traders. On this important point I would like to receive a report, as soon as possible, from each Mamludar.

> “ ‘16. Regarding the old and infirm, and those not able to work for a livelihood, I acknowledge with great pleasure the humane and liberal charity of which I hear accounts from all quarters, and I have such confidence in the people of this Zilla that I know the old and infirm will never be left to starve. Still, if this distress continues long, the demands on the private charity of the rich may exceed their means of meeting it, and I therefore wish the Mamludars to let me know whether they think any aid is required from elsewhere.

> “ ‘17. Till further orders, I request that a short report may be sent to me direct every Saturday by each Mamludar and Mahalkuree of the state of his Talooka, and the progress of works and the prospects of the crops.’

“7. The following table shows the rates of grain sold at Dharwar uring the distress :—


| Item | Per Rupee. |
| --- | --- |
| “ Jowaree | 6 seers. |
| “ Bajree | 5¼ do. |
| “ Rice, 1st sort | 4¼ do. |
| “ Do. 2nd do. | 6 do. |
| “ Dall | 4¼ do. |
| “ Koolthi | 6½ do. |
| “ Horse grain | 3½ do. |


<!-- page: 126 | folio: 114 -->
“ 8. The prices now prevailing are as shown below :—


| Per Rupee. | Column 2 | Column 3 |
| --- | --- | --- |
| “ Jowaree | 21 | Seers. |
| “ Bajree | 19½ | do. |
| “ Rice, 1st sort | 10 | do. |
| “ Do., 2nd do. | 11¼ | do. |
| “ Dall | 10½ | do. |
| “ Koolthi | 21 | do. |
| “ Horse grain | 5½ | do. |

“ 9. The harvest has been the best reaped for several seasons, and, owing to less land having been taken up for cotton, more cereals were cultivated, and the supply is now abundant.

“ 10. It may suffice to mention that the distress quite disappeared by December.

“ 11. The Collector has much pleasure of the opportunity of stating that, in addition to the aid afforded by Government in affording employment to the unemployed and poor, much private benevolence was displayed, wherever the distress prevailed, in the support of the young, old and infirm. The charitable exertions of the wealthier Natives and of entire communities were very timely, and the subjoined statement shows the extent to which charity was extended:—


| Towns or Villages. | Average number of persons fed daily, or amount expended. | Period for which fed. | Remarks. |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Dharwar | 600 to 700 persons | Three months. | The work of providing for the infirm and young was managed very satisfactorily by a committee composed principally of non-official Natives. The subscribers were of all classes, and gave their aid very willingly. |
| Hooblee | 1,251 Rupees | Do. | Besides the amount mentioned in column 2, Chunveerupa bin Chunbusupa Neelee, a Native trader, spent 1,500 Rupees in feeding the poor of Hooblee for four months. Gungapa bin Amhuna Bendigherry spent 540 Rs. in feeding the poor for three months. Four others, by name Siduppa Warkar, Sidhugapa Menchinkai, Chenupa Uddurgoonchi, and Udrepa Oogurgole, spent jointly Rs. 860 in feeding the poor for five months. Govindupa bin Soobupa Koomptee expended 150 Rs. in feeding the poor for two months. |

The principal donors of the balance were Sidramupa Sheftee; Mullupa Dharwar; Kasim Saheb Patel; Eurungowda; Bhimupa Bednal alone contributed Rs. 100. |

| Nurgoond ...... | 1,000 „ ............ | Do. ...... | The sum was given by Bussupa bin Gurryappa Junthlee of Nurgoond for repairs to a tank on the high road to Sholapoor, and thus provided labour for the unemployed poor. |
| Do. ...... | 475 „ ............ | Do. ...... | Contributed by Gopallupa bin Timmupa Auingoondee, a Sowkar of Nurgoond, for repairing a tank in the suburbs of Nurgoond, and thus provided labour for the poor. |
| Do. ....... | 500 persons............. | Two months ... | Rupees 1,000 were spent in feeding the infirm and aged. Principal donors Benkupa bin Kullianappa and Dod-Bussupa bin Basetapa Dawungheery. |
| Gadug............. | 916 Rupees............. | Do. ...... | In addition to the sum mentioned in column 2, much private charity was displayed. The principal donors were Mudiwalupa Boola (who for two months fed about 1000 people daily); Bugwuntupa Chitturgar; and Manvee Fukeerupa. Of the 916 Rs. mentioned in column 2 Rs. 230 were contributed from the Dharwar Station Famine Fund. |
| Dumbul ......... | 465 - „ ............ | Do. ...... | Of this sum Rs. 220 were contributed by the Dharwar Station Famine Fund. |
| Kurujghec....... | 100 „ ............ | Do. ...... | Spent in feeding the poor. |
| Havaree ......... | 500 „ ............ | Do. ...... | The number of persons mentioned in column 2 were reported to be fed daily at Havaree for two months by Sooli-hully Errupa Sowkar. |
| •Deveehosoor ... | 200 „ ............ | Do. ...... | Two hundred people were fed daily for two months at Deveehosoor by Buschetappa Khoorubgoondur Sowkar. |

<!-- page: 128 | folio: 116 -->
> "12. In conclusion, the Collector feels much satisfaction in stating that while the distress prevailed subscriptions were collected for the relief of the sufferers on the East Coast of India, and though under the sanction contained in Government Resolution No. 3568, dated 22nd September 1866, some of the funds collected were diverted for the relief of local distress, a remittance of over 3,700 Rupees has been made to the Secretaries in Bombay of the Bengal Famine Relief Fund."

54\. This completes the famine reports of the several Officers in charge of tracts of country above the Ghauts from the northern-most to the southern-most extremity of the Presidency. I will now proceed to record the statements of the few Collectors in charge of districts in the Concan, below the Ghauts, and between the Ghauts and the sea-board of the Western Coast.

55\. On 5th October the Acting Collector of Tannah, Mr. Arbuthnot, replied to my reference as follows :—

> "1. With reference to your Circular dated 1st July last, I have the honor to report that there are no records here to show the occurrence of any famine in this Collectorate.

> "2. From enquiries made from the old inhabitants it appears that a famine occurred in the year 1802 or 1803 in the up-country, probably in the district bordering on the Godavery River, called Gungthudee, when hundreds of famished people came into the Concan, and were fed by the private charity of the inhabitants. It had the effect of raising the prices of grain in these parts. Government assisted, it is said, by employing these men on the work of the Bombay and Tannah road then under construction. These labourers were allowed each a seer, or twenty-eight tolas, of rice and an old pice. Government also established a shop at Oorun, and rice brought from Bombay was sold there at one pailee a Rupee.

> "3. In 1824-25, owing to scanty rains, the crop yielded in Dhanoo, Bassein, and Salsette Talookas one-fourth the usual produce, and consequently great scarcity of grain prevailed. Rice and other grains were sold at famine prices. No casualties, however, occurred. The only assistance Government gave was the exemption from assessment, but there is no record to verify this.

> "4. In 1838-39 also, owing to the failure of rain in Salsette, the crops failed, but fortunately by a timely arrival of a supply of rice from Malabar the population was saved from the impending calamities."


<!-- page: 129 | folio: 117 -->
56\. I suggested to the Acting Collector that further minute enquiries should be made during the following three months, and that the result should be communicated at the end of that time.

57\. On 21st January last I received a further communication from the Tannah Acting Collector, Mr. Robertson, to the effect that further minute enquiry had elicited no additional information—that the few records which were forthcoming contained no notice whatever of famines or scarcities.

58\. On 6th September Mr. Salmon, the Acting Sub-Collector of Colaba, replied that—

> “ 2. There are no written records of any famine in the Sub-Collectorate, and there does not appear to have been such a serious scarcity of food since the introduction of the British rule as could properly be termed a famine. Considerable remissions to the extent of one-sixth to one-tenth of the revenue were granted in 1817-18 in the parts of the Sub-Collectorate under British rule, but no extraordinary measures of relief seem to have been called for, or resorted to, on the part of Government. Charitable Natives distributed food, but the scarcity only lasted a year, and was attended with no loss of life.

> “ 3. All over the Sub-Collectorate there is oral evidence of a great famine in the early part of the present century between 1801—1804. This extended over the Deccan from Malabar up to Guzerat. Commencing in the country above the Ghauts, the influx of people in search of food from there into the Concan, added to a scarcity of rain, and consequent partial failure of crops, caused the extension of this famine to these districts. All the oral information given states this to have been a very severe famine. Great numbers are said to have died, and children to have been sold for food. Rice is said to have sold for Rs. 400 a candy, and Tandool at 1 Rupee a seer, until rice was received from Bengal, when prices fell. Entire remission of the revenue while the famine lasted, which it is variously stated to have done for from eight months to two years, is said to have been given by the Peishwa's Government. Both Government and rich Natives are represented as having distributed food, and paid for the burning or interment of the dead. Government also supplied grain from the Government stores by ‘tugai.’ The export of grain was prohibited, all grain being ordered to be consumed in the village it was produced in. Raghojee Bawa Angria, who was then Chief of Colaba, used to give a ½ seer of Tandool to any one who threw down on the road a bundle of straw for the cattle.”


<!-- page: 130 | folio: 118 -->
59\. I also suggested more deliberation to Mr. Salmon, and pointed out that, even in the absence of written records, much valuable information might, perhaps, be gained orally by the Mamludars and others from private sources, or, at all events, that he would be in a better position to report upon the subject finally some months after; but with no better success.

60\. Mr. Boswell, the Rutnagherry Collector, on 20th September wrote that—

> “2. No English or Vernacular records of famines, either before or since the introduction of the British Government, are forthcoming, and the information I supply you with rests only on oral tradition; nor does it appear that any distress from failure of rain and crops, worthy of the name of famine, has occurred in this district under the present Government.

> “3. Reports regarding famines before the present rule have been called for from each Talooka, and it appears that there are only two famines of which the people preserve any traditions, and these are stated to have taken place about the years 1792 and 1802. They seem to have been general throughout the whole of this district.

> “4. In the three Northern Talookas of Sooverndroog, Anjunwell, and Rutnagherry, the famine of 1802 seems to have lasted twelve or fourteen months, and that of 1792 for eight or ten months. They were most severely felt in the Khed Petta, which, from being so jungly, is still about the poorest of the districts. The prices of rice seem to have been from 4 to 6 seers for the Rupee in 1802, and still dearer in 1792.

> “5. In the Southern Talookas the traditions of two famines are preserved, but the dates vary slightly. However, the years 1792 and 1802 seem to be the most probable dates. In the first famine in the Viziadroog Talooka there was scarcity of food for four months, and in the second for two or three months. According to the Rajapoor measure (1½ Rajapoor seers = 1 Rutnagherry seer), rice was sold at 6 seers the Rupee. In the Malwan Talooka the distress of 1802 seems to have been great, and to have lasted for more than a year. During the fair season rice was sold at 7 or 8 seers the Rupee, and during the monsoon for three months at 4 or 5 seers. This famine does not appear to have been so severely felt in the Salsee Petta.

> “6. There are nothing but rough guesses as to the decrease in the population from starvation and emigration. In Khed Petta it is said that the population was reduced by one-fourth in 1802. In Chiploon it is said [p. 119] that in the largest villages as many as 50 may have died, but generally no numbers are given. No villages were entirely deserted, and the famine appears to have carried off only those of the poorest classes.

7\. The results of the famine in the Malwan Soobha were that many people died, and eight villages were almost depopulated by death alone, and the few survivors went into the Goanese territory to seek sustenance. In the Vingorla Petta the distress was somewhat increased by the arrival of some 400 starving persons from beyond the Ghauts, but they were provided for by the liberality of the Raja of Waree.

“8. With regard to the earlier famine of 1792, little information is to be met with, but there can be no doubt that both this and the one of 1802 extended over the whole of this district, and that the latter was severest in the Khed Petta and Malwan Soobha.

“9. With regard to the measures of relief adopted, it appears that ‘Anchutras,’ or food-houses, were established by private persons, and the poorest of the people fed daily. A kind of thin gruel, made of ‘Naglec’ and water, was distributed thus to the people, but it is stated that this did not stay the distress. In a few places, and particularly in the Soverndroog Talooka, Government also established some few ‘Anchutras.’ The building now used as the Cutchcrry at Khed was up to a very recent period known by the name of ‘Anchutra,’ and this is referred to the famine of 1802. Owing to the stores of grain received by Government in payment of the rents and kept in various forts, there was probably more in hand to relieve distress with than would be found in the case of a similar visitation occurring now. It also appears that during the famine of 1802 the revenue for that year was, in some parts of the Southern Division of this district, left uncollected, and people were gradually induced to return to their homes and their cultivating operations, and to such the Government taxes were remitted. The Sowkar creditors were also ordered by Government not to exact money from their debtors among the starving population until times became more prosperous. For three years afterwards it is stated that Government did not collect the full revenue, but made great remissions wherever cultivation was recommenced.

“10. It should be remembered that the Concan is never likely to be so severely visited by famine as other districts, both because there is seldom so little rain as to cause an absolute failure of crops of all sorts, and also because every part of it is so easily accessible by water, and therefore grain can be easily introduced from any place where the scarcity is less severe, as long as the means of purchasing exist.

“11. Although, therefore, the scarcity of food in 1792 and 1802 throughout these districts is spoken of as famine, yet its results can hardly be [p. 120] said to have been disastrous, and under British Government absolute starvation from want is not very likely ever to occur to any great extent."

61\. Mr. Boswell also did me the favour to forward copy of a report dated 26th July 1826 addressed to Government by the Collector, Mr. Moore, relative to a partial failure of crops that year. It is transcribed below:—

> "1. I have the honor to acknowledge the receipt of Mr. Secretary Farish's letter dated 19th January last, calling for a report on the state of this Collectorate for the official year 1824-25, as required by the orders of Government of 1st May 1819.

> "2. As a full report on the state of these districts was transmitted by my predecessor on the 15th August 1824, and as I received charge of the Collectorate only two months before the expiration of the official year, it will probably be sufficient for me to state the measures which were adopted after the above report was written for the purpose of relieving the Ryots from the distress occasioned by the scantiness of the crops.

> "3. The rains of 1824, as Government has already been informed, were unusually light in the Concan, and caused a complete failure of the crops in high situations, and a partial failure in the low rice lands. The very high price of grain compensated in some degree for the scantiness of the produce, but the loss in general was very great; and as the preceding year (1823) has also been unfavourable it becomes necessary to grant large remissions of revenue.

> "4. An attempt at first was made to classify the villages according to the degree of loss which they had sustained, but as this was found to occupy too much time a general estimate was framed of the deficiency of the crops in the different kinds of land, and the following remissions were granted:—

> "I—The grain revenue was directed to be received in money at the Behara rate, and as the Bazar price of grain was double that of the Behara this was of itself a great relief to all those who possessed grain.

> "II—But as many persons could not benefit by this arrangement, having no grain to dispose of, the following remission was granted to all those whose payments were under 5 candies:—

- "One-third was remitted on Awul land.
- "Two-thirds ditto on Doem, and other rice lands.
- "Four-fifths ditto on Wurkus, or dry land. [p. 121] “To all whose payments above 5 candies, and who might, therefore, be supposed to benefit by the high price of grain— “One-sixth was remitted on Awul land. “One-third ditto on Doem land. “Two-fifths ditto on Wurkus land.
> “5. Some variations were made in these rates according to the circumstances of particular districts. In the Ryghur Talooka the different sorts of land not being distinguished, a remission of one-third was granted to all paying under 5 candies, and of one-fifth to those paying above that quantity.

> “6. The whole of these remissions amounted to Rs. 4,23,678, and, I believe, gave general satisfaction to the people : they were larger than had ever been granted by the late Government, but not more than necessary, and in consequence of the relief they afforded the revenue of the past year was realized without any unusual difficulty.

> “7. It is probable that in some instances the remissions had not reached those for whom they were intended, but every means was used to inform the people of the amount to which they were entitled. Proclamations were issued, stating the remission which had been given, and in Coolarugee villages a statement of each person’s payment and the amount of abatement was posted up in a public part of the village.

> “8. Besides the relief which was thus afforded, the Mamludars were authorized, agreeably to the sanction of Government, to give employment on a public work to all persons who are willing to work for a mere subsistence, but the people did not avail themselves of this indulgence, except in the Ryghur and Sanksay Talookas, and the majority of those who were there employed were inhabitants of the Deccan, who had come down to the Concan in consequence of the difficulty of obtaining subsistence above the Ghauts.

> “9. Several tanks were cleaned out and deepened in the neighbourhood of Mahar and at Penn, some of which had been long without repair, that it would have been necessary to have expended a considerable sum for their improvement, even if there had not been the additional object of giving employment to the people.”

62\. Lieutenant Colonel Schneider, the Political Agent in the Sawunt Warree District, replied to my reference as follows on 27th August :—

> “1. I have the honor to acknowledge the receipt of your Circular letter of the 1st ultimo, and in reply to forward copy of a Yadee drawn up [p. 122] at my desire by the principal hereditary officer embodying such information as he has been able to gather from records and from private individuals as to periods of distress from scarcity of food in this principality. “2. The first instance given dates back to <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1792-93, when rice rose to the extraordinary rate of 3 to 5 seers ; Naglee 8, and Jowaree and Ooreed 9 seers per Peerkhanee Rupee. (This Rupee has been assayed and valued at 87 Rupees per 100 : the seer is 60 Tolas, or 1½ lbs. English.) “3. The causes of this scarcity seem to have been— “ ‘1. The frequent devastation of districts and villages by the forces of the neighbouring Kolapoor Raja. The latter in person invaded Sawunt Warree in 1787-88, and plundered and laid the villages waste in his progress. “ ‘2. A drought in 1791-92, and consequent failure of crops. There is nothing to show whether this latter misfortune was general, but the area of the State is small, and I should doubt there ever being any great difference between the rain-fall of our district and the others. A drought in the South Concan would take in Sawunt Warree.’ “4. The remedy adopted seems to have been to waylay certain rice laden vessels, and distribute their cargoes through the country where the greatest distress prevailed. “5. In 1821-22 the price of rice rose to 6 seers per Rupee, the cause being a sudden and unusual fall of rain, accompanied by a terrific storm ; the former destroying the ground crops, and the latter the Bagayut produce. “6. On this occasion the Raja opened the State grain “Kotte,” and distributed supplies where most needed. “7. To my mind neither of the instances above given can be construed into famine, but the times were doubtless very hard.”

63\. On further reference, Colonel Schneider regretted his inability to give additional particulars.

64\. Mr. Elphinston, the Acting Collector of North Canara, reported on 14th December as follows :—

> “1. In reply to your Circular dated the 1st of July, I have the honor to report that, though North Canara has on different occasions suffered from a scarcity, the only one which, from its duration, extent, and the area of country affected, can be considered to have been a famine was in the year <span class="sc">a. d.</span> 1806, or the Ksheya Sumwatsara. “2. The old records of this Zilla are still at Mangalore, but reports show this famine to have been very severe : men were reduced to feed on [p. 123] roots in the jungle and on husks of rice: about 3,000 persons are said to have died of starvation and want. The famine was not confined to this district, but extended to Rutnagherry, Malwan, and Punderpoor.
