> _[Margin: Summary of Proceedings respecting grain from 1803 till the present period.]_
> _[Margin: Abrogation in September 1802 of the Custom Master's powers respecting the restriction of the export of grain.]_
> _[Margin: Custom Master authorised in September 1803 to put a stop to the export of grain till further orders.]_
> _[Margin: Explanation of the materials whence the following narrative is deduced.]_
> _[Margin: Liberal suggestions of Mr. Henshaw in respect to the means of alleviating the scarcity.]_

1\. Having had occasion to revise our proceedings on the matter of the Company's Counsel's Report of the 10th of May last, and of Mr. Henshaw's explanatory letter of the 8th instant, I have prepared the following summary, as well for the satisfaction of my own mind as to bring the whole subject more distinctly into a connected point of view. It will thence appear that after the abrogation of the Custom Master's general powers to regulate the export of grain, which took place by a Resolution of Council passed on the 3rd of September 1802, it was represented to us by Mr. Henshaw, in his capacity of Custom Master, on the 10th of September 1803, that in consequence of the failure of that season, unless a total prohibition were put to the exportation of rice, an inevitable famine would be the consequence; to which exportation he was accordingly authorised to put a stop, till further orders, by the Secretary's letter to him of the same month and year, after which he continued sometimes to address the Board, but more frequently the Governor, in his separate capacity; in consequence of which the records will show that I delivered in several of his letters to myself for the opinion of Government, whilst others of them that related chiefly to the execution and details incident to the powers with which he had been invested by Government appear to have been kept in my office, and replied to, as far as necessary, under my own signature, or by my order, of which separate correspondence, as well as of those notes and intimations that I have, in the like view of expediting the duties of Mr. Henshaw's charge in the grain department, had occasion to transmit to him; copies of such as have not already come before the Board are now delivered in for record, as far as I have been able to trace and collect them; and although every line or note may not be found in this series, yet all that is in the least degree material will, I fancy, be found to be so, or otherwise Mr. Henshaw can supply any that may be here deficient: and, meanwhile, I request that [p. 195] these may stand entered on the face of our proceedings, immediately after Mr. Henshaw's letter of the 8th of June, and having thus rendered them of record, the following narrative will be taken from the former and present materials, according to the order of dates, after generally referring to several of these separate letters or notes between me, or my Secretary, and Mr. Henshaw, and Mr. Halliday, the Superintendent of Police, that may be in the Board's recollection as standing already on record, relating chiefly to the precautions taken on the first threatening appearances of scarcity; one of which (in a letter from Mr. Henshaw of the 27th September 1803, as recorded in our proceedings of the 30th of that month) adverts to the exemption from town duties of ghee, oil, turmeric, jagree, molasses, chelee-pepper, and pawn; and that letter is farther recommendatory of the taking off of all duties on imports from Caranja and Salsette to Bombay, as well as the duties on all imports from the Continent to those Islands, and of the abolition of the petty customs on several indispensibly necessary articles, used principally by the poorer classes on this Island; Mr. Henshaw concluding on this occasion with the following expression—"In respect to myself as Custom Master, I beg to assure you I have only the public good in view, that I will labour hard to that end, nor think any sacrifice of my emoluments or time too great or ill-applied when it can be promoted thereby."

> _[Margin: Abolition of certain duties thereon proclaimed.]_

2\. In pursuance of these suggestions, an advertisement was issued on the 27th of September 1803, announcing the several exemptions suggested in Mr. Henshaw's preceding recommendations.

> _[Margin: Superintendent of Police appointed to dispose of grain for the consumption of the Islands.]_

3\. Mr. Henshaw having farther recommended on the same occasion that the Garrison Storekeeper should be directed to bring to sale 200 bags of rice daily, in small limited quantities, and at a certain price, a committee was appointed—of himself, the Superintendent of Police, and of Captain Moor, the Garrison Storekeeper—to report thereon, which they did in recommendation of the plan, but preferring to delegate the charge of it to the Superintendent of Police, instead of the Garrison Storekeeper, as was accordingly sanctioned on the 30th of September 1803.

> _[Margin: Means taken to procure rice from Mangalore, &c.]_
> _[Margin: Honorable Company's ship *Elphinstone* sent to Bengal for rice.]_

4\. On the 18th and 19th of September 1803 applications had been made by Government to Mr. Ravenshaw, the Collector of Mangalore, respecting the sending up rice for this market, in pursuance of which the *Huddart* was on the 12th of October dispatched thither for its conveyance; [p. 196] and under the same date Major (now Lieutenant Colonel) Malcolm, the Resident at Mysore being then at this place, wrote to his Assistant, Mr. Peile, to promote this object, by corresponding with Mr. Ravenshaw, and by encouraging the transit of grain from Mysore to Mangalore, as far as might not interfere with the arrangements made by the Honorable General Wellesley for the supplies of his Army, or other military objects in that quarter; whilst, as a farther measure of precaution, the Board determined on the 23rd of September 1803 to dispatch the *Elphinstone* to Bengal for a cargo of rice.

> _[Margin: Mr. Henshaw authorised to buy rice, dealing liberally with the importers.]_
> _[Margin: Allusion to the state of the treasury.]_
> _[Margin: Reliance expressed in Mr. Henshaw's honor, zeal, and judgment.]_

5\. In my separate letter to Mr. Henshaw of the 15th of October 1803, in answer to one from him of the preceding date, I expressed to him, in view to the measures that had been thus pursued, my hope that he would now be able to keep us clear of famine, and gradually to reduce the price of rice, adding that he might buy all the rice that came in, as long as he should find it useful; giving liberal prices, so as that the importers might not be discouraged in pursuing the traffic, and taking care not to force their inclinations even in obliging them to sell to Government when they might prefer to deal with others, since we could always afford to give as much as any first purchaser from the importer; and noticing that although our treasury was exceedingly low, yet if he wanted a small sum to go on with to let me know, and he should have it, for that the importers should be paid ready cash; and concluding by an assurance that I entrusted this great and delicate commission to his honor, zeal, and judgment, and relied on experiencing the best effects from the confidence thus placed.

> _[Margin: Mr. Henshaw's proposition to buy the rice imported by individuals from Mangalore.]_
> _[Margin: His assurances of an honourable discharge of this duty.]_
> _[Margin: Directed to keep the shops of the Superintendent of Police supplied for the local consumption.]_

6\. On the same date, but before he had received my last mentioned letter, Mr. Henshaw wrote another, advising that 1,200 robins of rice had just entered from Mangalore, the owner of which had sold it to the second man, provided Government permitted it, such second man having been with Mr. Henshaw, and declared his willingness to give up his purchase for two annas profit on each robin, which Mr. Henshaw recommended as being likely to produce the best consequences, as well as that all future imports during the next thirty days should be purchased in the same manner; and concluding with these words —"I will manage it for your comfort and honor, believe me." In acknowledging which I, on the same day, expressed my entire [p. 197] approbation of his purchasing the rice then imported, and selling it in such manner as he should see the most likely to produce the greatest general relief; adding “I have told Mr. Halliday that I leave it to you to supply his shops; he will speak to you on the subject.”

> _[Margin: Mr. Henshaw's farther assurances of a conscientious discharge on his part of the duty thus reposed in him, and without calling on the treasury if he could avoid it.]_

7\.  In his next letter of the day following, after acknowledging mine of the 15th, he writes—“I shall conscientiously endeavour to meet your wishes in the discharge of the trust you have reposed in me, and to merit your regards, and you may depend on my exerting every means to save calling on the treasury for assistance.”

> _[Margin: Explanation as to what parts of the separate correspondence will be noticed in this narrative.]_

8\.  Under dates the 2nd and 3rd of December 1803 there will be found in the collection of separate correspondence now delivered in some notice of payments into the treasury, and of the straitened state of our resources, which not appearing very material to the subject under consideration, need not here be recapitulated; a rule which, for the sake of requisite brevity, will be observed in respect to all the other more irrelative notices it may be found to contain.

> _[Margin: Mr. Henshaw consulted about the growing scarcity at Poona, and the proposition from that quarter to send down their Brinjarries to purchase from Bombay.]_
> _[Margin: Notice of Mr. Henshaw's having recommended a proportion of the imports to be exported; and query to him thereon, with caution to him to keep an eye on the market, so as to provide for our own exterior and interior wants.]_
> _[Margin: His consequent suggestion to extend our purchases.]_

9\.  On the 14th of December 1803 I advised Mr. Henshaw of my having heard (no doubt from the Resident) at Poona that grain was getting very scarce at that capital, and that the Government there wished to send down the Native Brinjarries to purchase for the community at that place, upon which I asked him whether any rice had yet been re-exported out of the one-third proportion that he recommended; adding “I rely on your keeping your eye on the market with the view to provide always, *1st*, for our armies above the ghauts, to the extent of General Wellesley's commission of 1,000 garce; and *2nd*, for our population here;” and thereon suggesting to him the expediency of buying another ship's cargo; in answer to which Mr. Henshaw suggested the purchasing to the extent of one lac of bags, in addition to what he had previously engaged for, in view not only to the supply of the Poona Brinjarries, but to give us the means of exerting the proper checks over the markets in Bombay, and thereby to protect the poor from (according to Mr. Henshaw's expression) “those harpies, the grain dealers.”


<!-- page: 210 | folio: 198 -->
> _[Margin: Mr. Henshaw's proposal in December 1803 to extend the indulgence of export to half of the import.]_

10\. Under date the 24th of December 1803 Mr. Henshaw addressed to me a further letter, which in view, no doubt, to its importance, I appear to have placed on the public record, as it suggested the propriety of extending, till further orders, the indulgence of export to half of the import, which would, he observed, increase the price to what would make the trip from Bengal a gain, and not, as was then the case, a loss ; Government's acquiescence in which proposition was on the same day signified to him by the Secretary.

> _[Margin: Mr. Henshaw's application to be allowed an Assistant and Purvoe in the Grain Department.]_
> _[Margin: Answer.]_

11\. On the 29th December 1803 Mr. Henshaw wrote to me that he had hitherto managed to bear the Honorable Company harmless as to every expense for assistance, Purvoes, &c., for the Grain Department, but that he now found it pressed very hard upon him, and to become even burthensome, so as to induce him to request I would permit him to employ Mr. Whitehill to assist him, with one good Purvoe ; and as they would have very full employment, he hoped I would not think him extravagant in recommending Mr. Whitehill for a monthly allowance of 200 Rupees, and a Purvoe for one of 50 Rupees ; to which I on the same day replied that as long as there was a gentleman in the service unprovided for, I could not consent to the employment of Mr. Whitehill, but that if he (Mr. Henshaw) liked to fix on any of the young men in the line of full employment, he should be named.

> _[Margin: Rise of the price of rice in the Bazar, and Mr. Henshaw's suggestion thereon to throw more into the market.]_
> _[Margin: Answer : that he was left to judge the regulation of the market.]_

12\. On the 22nd of January 1804 Mr. Henshaw advised me that the price of Moongee rice had risen considerably since last week, whence he judged it would be advisable to throw from 4 to 6,000 bags into the market, in order to prevent its further rise, provided I approved of the measure ; in reply to which I informed him on the same day that, as I left him to judge of the regulation of the market, I approved entirely of his intention to throw from 4 to 6,000 bags into the local consumption, by way of keeping the rice from rising too high.

> _[Margin: Proceedings in February 1804, in view to settling a reward for Mr. Henshaw's trouble.]_

13\. Under date the 7th of February 1804 there appears a letter from me to Mr. Henshaw, desiring his sentiments on what might constitute an eligible means, by commission or otherwise, of extending a fair and reasonable remuneration to him and to Mr. Ravenshaw, who purchased rice for us at Mangalore, without too great [p. 199] any answer from Mr. Henshaw till the 15th of June 1804, wherein, after referring to an intermediate report of the 23rd of April, he states the purchase made by him up to the latest date to amount to the value of 8,50,000 Rupees, observing that their future amount can be but trifling, and the “ large supplies in expectance are all upon contracts, some for small agreements recently made excepted, whose amount can (he adds) scare reach to two lacs ;” upon which data the Board recorded it as their opinion on the 15th of June that Mr. Henshaw would derive a due and sufficiently liberal reward by being allowed to charge 2½ per cent. both on his purchases and on his sales, with which Mr. Henshaw declared, in his letter of the 16th of June, his highest satisfaction, as well as at the Board’s expression, on the same occasion, of approbation of his conduct in the important trust committed to him.

14\.   But to resume the narrative according to the order of the dates.

> _[Margin: Mr. Henshaw’s suggestion in February 1804 to send the surplus rice intended for General Welles-ley for the local consumption of Poona, &c.]_
> _[Margin: Answer : Government leaving to his judgment to propose the apportionment of the home sales and exports.]_

The Honorable Sir Arthur Wellesly having declined the further supply of rice intended to have been sent to Poona, Mr. Henshaw proposed, in a letter to the Secretary to Government of the 16th of February, to bring the same, and also the arranged supply in aid of Poona, into market in regulated portions, and for exportation to Poona and the adjacent countries, “ having (as Mr. Henshaw concludes) ever in view the objects pointed out to him by Government ;” in reply to which it was, on the 18th of the same month and year, signified to him that

Government left to his judgment to propose the appointment of the rice to be sold here, and of the quantity to be exported, having always in view to guard the Company from loss throughout the whole concern, and on the 22nd his proposed appropriations thereof, both for home consumption and for exportation, received the sanction of Government.

> _[Margin: His proposed export to Surat.]_
> _[Margin: Mr. Henshaw’s suggestion agreed to on the 22nd February 1804, reducing the privilege of the export of grain to one-fourth of the imports of rice, &c. except-ing to Surat, whither one moiety might be sent.]_

15\.   The next notice that appears on the public records is a letter to me from Mr. Henshaw, proposing, under date the 21st February 1804, immediate transmission of 10,000 bags of rice to the Magistrate at Surat, in consequence of the great alarm that had arisen there as to the probable extent of the increasing scarcity in that quarter, to which, on the day following, the Board agreed, as well as to a farther suggestion that in his aforesaid letter Mr. Henshaw had submitted to me of a reduction of the exports to one-fourth of the imports of rice and other grain, excepting to Surat, where they should be allowed, under regulation, to the extent of onehalf, which orders issuing from the Board, although the letter was addressed [p. 200] to me, comes in further illustration of the principe that I have already explained to have generally regulated my conduct in respect to my intercourse on grain subjects with Mr. Henshaw.

> _[Margin: Mr. Henshaw's report on the 13th of March of a large quantity of grain being nearly smuggled off of the Islands, and proceedings thereon.]_
> _[Margin: Connection of the subject with the charge against his Purvoe in the Court.]_

16\. On the 13<sup>th</sup> of March 1804 Mr. Henshaw reported his detention of a large quantity of grain on the eve of being smuggled off of the Island, such as rendered it liable to forfeiture; but as he truly thought the proprietors were not aware of the crime they committed, he proposed levying a fine of half a rupee per bag on the grain in question, which was agreed to on the 15th of the same month and year, accompanied by a requisition to the Custom Master to submit a more detailed report on the subject, which as he omitted to make till the circumstances became an object of animadversion in the late trial of his Purvoe, Appajee Bhow, in the Court of Oyer and Terminer, this matter remains yet, in some degree, undetermined.

> _[Margin: Renewed notice from Poona of the scarcity.]_
> _[Margin: Desire expressed to obtain assistance from Bombay.]_
> _[Margin: Answer.]_
> _[Margin: Instructions thereon to Mr. Henshaw.]_
> _[Margin: Publication of the 30th of March 1804, permitting the exportation to the Continent of one moiety of all grain imported from the northward, and of one quarter of all imports from the southward.]_

17\. Under date the 24th of March 1804 a letter was received from Mr. Waring, the Assistant Secretary at Poona, signifying that His Highness the Paishwa had intimated the most earnest and anxious desire that the prohibition on the exportation of grain from the Island of Bombay should, if practicable, be withdrawn for the purpose of affording some supplies to that place, where numbers were dying daily from actual want; in acknowledging which communication the Secretary was, under date the 30th of March, instructed to advise Mr. Waring that there had not been any general prohibition on the exportation of grain from Bombay, which was allowed to be exported in certain specified proportions, the regulating or superintending of which was necessarily left to the Custom Master, who was at the same time informed of this correspondence with Mr. Waring, with instructions to be attentive that the purport of this notice to him should be attended to, so as to have due operation within the present prescribed limits of exportation in favour of the urgent necessities of the population at Poona; and a public advertisement was at the same time issued, announcing that Government had, until further orders, been pleased to permit the exportation to the Continent of one moiety of all grain imported by individuals from the northward, and of one quarter of all [p. 201] which proportions permits would be granted on application to the Custom Master.

> _[Margin: Mr. Henshaw's report on the 3rd of April 1804 that 1,40,000 bags of rice might be exported to Poona.]_
> _[Margin: Consequent order of the 5th of April 1804 to allow the Paishwa's Vakeels and the Poona Agents to export at once the amount of their purchases.]_
> _[Margin: Mr. Henshaw directed to reserve always in store 50,000 bags over and above every order or permission to export.]_

18\. In acknowledging the above instructions, Mr. Henshaw reported, on the 3rd of April 1804, that the stock on hand and ascertained expectancies enabled him to suggest that, unless very unforeseen circumstances arose to prevent it, an exportation would be permitted to Poona of 1,40,000 bags of rice, of which Mr. Waring was accordingly advised on the 5th of the same month (through Amerchund Bedurchund, who had brought his letter); the Custom Master being at the same time informed that, as his report had shown so considerable a surplus of rice to be applicable for the relief of Poona, the Governor in Council was pleased to direct that he allow the Paishwa's Vakeels and the ascertained Agents from Poona to export at once what they had already purchased, provided our actual stock on hand would safely admit thereof, and noting it as so much in the whole quantity that he had calculated to be allowed for export to that capital that would thus experience some immediate relief; and on the 6th of April the Custom Master was, in continuation of the same general subject, desired to keep in store a quantity of not less than 50,000 bags, understanding that the exports already authorised were to take place from what was surplus to that quantity.

> _[Margin: Committee proposed to be appointed the better to ensure this last object.]_
> _[Margin: Irritation and solemn declaration of Mr. Henshaw on that occasion.]_
> _[Margin: Idea of the committee thereon not prosecuted.]_

19\. For greater security in that respect Government thought fit, on the date last quoted, to appoint a committee, composed of Mr. Cowart and Captain Moor, to make a monthly report of the quantity of rice under the charge of the Custom Master, which irritated Mr. Henshaw, who observes, in his letter of the 13th of April, that he thence views himself as a suspected character, and that it had caused him to "review the proceedings had since he was first intrusted with this delicate and important commission : the orders" (adds this gentleman) "transmitted to me, with their replies, have received a close revision from me; the purchases, the sales made by me, my cash transactions, the expenses I have incurred on account of the trust, all have passed a close and conscientious investigation, and I feel I should be wanting to myself if I for a moment longer scrupled to avow, in this most public manner, that I have been actuated solely by an anxious unremitted zeal for the public good; nor for a moment ever suffered a private [p. 202] consideration to influence or draw me aside from that great object; *that I have neither derived for myself, or suffered others, directly or indirectly, to derive the smallest advantage from the trust reposed in me, or confided by me to them; that I have violated no order of Government, or ever presumed to act upon the suggestions of my own mind, unless first sanctioned by their approval of the measure*; this avowal thus publicly made, I must hope that the Honorable the Governor in Council will not be displeased at my humble request to be relieved from making any comment or remark on the reference, but will do me the justice to believe that the same zeal that first prompted me to offer my assistance to Government in this department still occupies my best faculties to the total exclusion of every other object;" which representation had such an effect upon Government that they dispensed with the institution of the Commission in question.

> _[Margin: Free trade between Salsette and Bombay.]_
> _[Margin: Complaint of Amerchund Boderchund, a merchant, of not being permitted to export his Poona purchases.]_
> _[Margin: Stress laid by Mr. Henshaw on the Government Regulations.]_
> _[Margin: Orders thereon.]_

20\. On the 27th of April 1803 it was notified to Mr. Henshaw by the Secretary to Government, in corroboration of a former order, that there was to be a free trade between Salsette and Bombay, unburthened with tolls or customs on rice, or any other article. On the same date a petition was preferred by Amerchund Boderchund, merchant at Poona, then residing at Bombay (of whom mention is made in the 18th paragraph), setting forth that, agreeably to the orders of His Highness the Paishwa, he had, about three months ago, purchased about 1,000 candies of grain of different sorts for the consumption of the city of Poona, which having reported to His Highness, the latter had since enclosed to this petitioner a letter from Colonel Close, the Resident at Poona, to the address of Mr. Secretary Grant, which having delivered he received Mr. Grant's answer to the address of Colonel Close; and having forwarded the same to the Paishwa, the latter had transmitted to him in consequence a paper, under the official authentication of Colonel Close, certifying that the bearer was a merchant at Poona, authorised by His Highness the Paishwa to proceed to Bombay to purchase grain for the consumption of that city, notwithstanding which Mr. Henshaw signified to him that he could not admit of the export, as this grain had not been purchased from the Honorable Company; which petition having been referred to Mr. Henshaw for his report, he advised the Secretary, in his letter of the 8th of May 1804, that on the petitioner's first arriving and presenting Colonel Close's certificate the Custom Master had acquainted him of the order of Government in regard to the aid to Poona, and had decidedly given him to understand that there were certain regulations adopted by Government for the export of grain [p. 203] which on no account whatever would be deviated from—" that if he could so arrange his purchases as to meet these regulations he would experience no obstacles whatever ;" adding that " on referring to the Custom House exports and imports of grain not one man of the many the petitioner named had any balance due to justify any further export, and consequently it was in direct breach of the Government orders if one grain of the above was permitted to be exported, and would lead to evils of the most incalculable magnitude to our own Islands and to our Dependencies ; and though I feel every desire to give full effect to Colonel Close's certificates, and to relieve the distresses of the capital and country of His Highness the Paishwa, yet my primary duty to this Government compels me to view its orders and arrangements as paramount to every personal feeling and anxious wish to relieve our neighbours. I cannot, therefore, but press on the Honorable the Governor in Council a rejection of the petition," which was adopted by Government, as signified to the Custom

Master by Mr. Secretary Grant on the 10th of May, and Colonel Close was advised accordingly.

> _[Margin: Colonel Close's letter in favour of Khundee Row Rasta.]_
> _[Margin: Mr. Henshaw's remarks thereon.]_
> _[Margin: Order thereon.]_

21\. On the 29th of April 1804 Colonel Close represented, in a letter to the Secretary to the Government, that there were three boats laden with grain by the agents of Khundee Row Rasta in some of the market Ports in Guzerat, that having arrived in Bombay were prevented from prosecuting their voyage to Panwell, which the Colonel suggested they might be, and that the quantity might, if necessary, be included in that allowed for exportation to Poona during the present season ; which having been also referred to Mr. Henshaw, that gentleman observed that Government had been sorely troubled with mis-statements and mis-representations, to relieve them from which, and believing their wishes to be in concurrence with the Resident at Poona's request as to the grain in question, if to be done consistently, he begged to suggest that the quantity of joaree landed from these three botellas might be permitted to be exported to Panwell, as a *mark of attention to the Paishwa*, which was agreed to, and Mr. Henshaw advised on the same date as in respect to the subject of the preceding paragraph.

> _[Margin: Upon the case of Hurry Bhugtee's charity grain.]_

22\. A further letter from the Resident at Poona under date the 1st May was in like manner referred to Mr. Henshaw for his report. This application from Colonel Close recited that the Soucar, Samul Hurry Bhugtee, had represented to him that his agent at Baroda had some time [p. 204] since purchased by his desire 100 pullas of coarse grain, intended to be distributed in charity at that place, which, being also detained at Bombay, the Resident requested it might be allowed to be exported to Panwell, and the quantity included in that allowed for exportation to Poona during the current season, respecting which last cargo Mr. Henshaw reported, on the same date, that he had yesterday granted to the agent of Hurry Bhugtee free export thereof, on the score and certain information of its being devoted to charitable uses at Poona.

> _[Margin: Mr. Henshaw's suggestion on the 15th of May that the export of all grain, rice excepted, should be prohibited.]_
> _[Margin: This prohibition rendered general on the 23rd of May 1804.]_

23\. On the 15th of May 1804 the Custom Master submitted to the Governor in Council that the export of all grain, rice excepted, should be prohibited from that day, as was accordingly sanctioned by Mr. Secretary Grant's answer of the day following ; and on the 23rd of the same month and year this prohibition was, at the further suggestion of Mr. Henshaw of the 22nd of that month, extended to rice also. The causes for this general prohibition are entered into more fully in Mr. Henshaw's separate letter to me of the 8th of June, stating that when he suggested the general stop to the exportation of any grain whatsoever the stock in store was low, whilst only one ship of the first convoy from Bengal had arrived, nor was there any room to expect the others before the middle of June.

> _[Margin: A further application from Colonel Close in favour of a Carcoon to be allowed to export grain to Poona.]_
> _[Margin: Application thereon by Mr. Henshaw (24th May 1804) for a discretionary power to authorise the export of grain to Poona on the present or former pass notes of the Resident at that capital.]_
> _[Margin: Export to Poona thus applied for allowed on the 25th May 1804, on condition of reserving always the stock of 50,000 bags in store.]_

24\. Under date the 28th of April a letter was addressed to the Secretary to Government by the Resident at Poona, setting forth that the bearer of it was a Carcoon belonging to that Durbar, who proceeded to Bombay to purchase 200 candies of grain, respecting which the Resident observed that if it should be inconvenient to allow the Carcoon to remove from Bombay the above-mentioned quantity the Colonel would request that he might be permitted to purchase such a proportion of it as could with propriety be spared, including it in that of which the exportation to Poona during the present season had been declared by Government to be allowed : which being referred to Mr. Henshaw for his report, he noticed, under date the 24th of May, that it had escaped him in his letters to the Secretary, respecting the prohibition on the export of grain and rice, to state that he by no means meant to bar the doors to any small exports that could consistently be admitted in aid of His Highness, and forming part of the quantity noted to the Resident at Poona, and was, therefore, induced to suggest that Government would please to vest in him a discretionary [p. 205] power to authorise, consistently with existing circumstances, the export to Poona of the quantity now applied for, as well as any small balances that might remain over for some former pass notes from the Resident at that Court; in answer to which it was signified to Mr. Henshaw, on the 28th of May, "that as long as he could keep the 50,000 bags. in store Government can admit of the desired export to Poona, but otherwise not."

> _[Margin: The former freedom of export restored on the 8th of June 1804.]_
> _[Margin: Mr. Henshaw's suggestion to sell a certain quantity of the Company's grain for exportation.]_

25\. In a subsequent letter to me from Mr. Henshaw of the 8th of June (already adverted to in the 23rd preceding paragraph) that gentleman suggests the means that had occurred to him of extending relief to Poona (respecting which the Supreme Government had also expressed their solicitude) at prices more favorable than were understood then to prevail there, to which end he proposed that the embargo laid on the one-fourth proportion of imports might be taken off; and that Government should announce the opening of their own stores at certain moderate prices, of which Colonel Close should be advised, that he might concert with the minister the best means of availing themselves thereof; and Mr. Henshaw further proposed that, as an additional check, the supplies of grain thus to be parted with should only be made "to those who bring certificates from Colonel Close to that end." On these suggestions from Mr. Henshaw, it was accordingly notified to the public on the same date (8th June 1804) that the letter and spirit of the Government advertisement of the 30th March were again in force, of which the Resident at Poona was also advised and furnished with a copy of Mr. Henshaw's letter of the 8th for his sentiments. Soon afterwards, viz. on the 12th of the same month, the Custom Master suggested the propriety of opening the Honorable Company's grain stores for the sale of from 10 to 20,000 bags of rice for exportation, and that its price should be of Moonghy rice Rupees 12, and of Patcherree rice Rupees 13 per bag; in consequence of which Mr. Henshaw was on the same date authorized to sell as far as 5,000 bags at the price and for the purpose he mentioned, which might, it was remarked, operate beneficially on the exportation intended to be allowed to the importers during the embargo of their one-fourth respectively, to which he should accordingly give immediate effect, which might (as was further noticed) render any larger present export by the Company unnecessary, and enable Government to save the Honorable Company's store of rice as much as possible.


<!-- page: 218 | folio: 206 -->
> _[Margin: Case of an application for chenna under a rahdary from General Wellesley.]_
> _[Margin: Caution to Mr. Henshaw.]_
> _[Margin: Testimony in his favour.]_

26\. Under dates the 16th and 17th of June 1804 there will be found in the collection of my separate correspondence with Mr. Henshaw a discussion with him respecting an application on account of Captain Barclay, Deputy Adjutant General with General Wellesley, to export a quantity of chenna, for which his servant had brought a rahdary, or passport under the seal of General Wellesley, which Mr. Henshaw appears to have mistaken for an antiquated one of the Paishwa's, or otherwise he would, he said, have first submitted to my judgment the authority for the application, and been guided by my orders; “but I trust (continues he) none of them, for there are hundreds of notes daily presented, in gentlemen's names, which I ever disregard, unless accompanied with a letter to me as Custom Master.” In reference to this special rahdary, I desired that the eight bullock loads of chenna should be allowed to be exported “if the quantity in town would, in view to the season and our local calls for that grain, admit thereof,” in Mr. Henshaw's opinion, but not otherwise; that is, not to our own material distress. On this occasion, after intimating to Mr. Henshaw that he seemed too inaccessible too much to his Purvoes, I added—“ I know you have more to do than you can well get through, and I believe you as fit or fitter for the weighty trust reposed in you than any one that could have been chosen for it.”

> _[Margin: Application on behalf of the Peishwa for grain, &c., for his own consumption.]_
> _[Margin: Of the late Killedar of Loghur.]_
> _[Margin: Joint report thereon by the Custom Master and Superintendent of Police.]_

27\. Under date the 28th of May a letter was written by Colonel Close, the Resident at Poona, intimating his hope that the late arrivals of ships from Bengal might admit the exportation of a further quantity of grain to Poona, which induced him to request that the person accompanying this letter might be permitted to purchase ghee and grain of different sorts to the amount of twenty thousand Rupees, as required by His Highness the Paishwa for the consumption of his own household. This was followed by a letter from Dhondoo Ballaul, the late Killedar of Loghur, applying under the auspices of General Wellesley for permission to buy grain towards the support of the late Nana Furnavees' family under his charge, and who had, with himself, recently come under the Company's protection. Both these applications were on the 12th of June referred by the Board for the joint report of Messrs. Henshaw and Halliday as to whether so much grain could be spared, in consequence of which these gentlemen reported, on the 18th of June, that all the articles in both might be complied with, excepting the bajree and joaree, “articles that would not (they observed) admit of [p. 207] any export, consistently with the existing regulations as to all exports," of all which Colonel Close was advised as far as respected the Paishwa, as was General Wellesley in regard to the part that related to his protegé Dhondoo Ballaul. With respect to that part of the above report which states that neither bajree nor joaree could be allowed to be sent at that period out of the Island, “consistently with the existing regulations as to all exports,”

> _[Margin: Stock of northern grain not admitting of export.]_
> _[Margin: Paishwa and Killedar of Loghur supplied from the Company’s stock as matter of favour.]_

the key to that observation may be probably found in a report from the Custom Master of the day before on similar subjects, wherein he states that “the stock in store of these grains will not admit of any export whatever therefrom, but that the full quantity of 200 ghautee candies of rice (which the Soubahdar of Chowl had applied for) might be permitted to be exported by him consistently with the existing regulations of its forming part of some privileged export of a fourth of some actual import, but not otherwise, unless Government should please to admit the purchase from their store and at their export price, which mode was accordingly similarly recommended, and resorted to on the day following, in favour of the Paishwa and Killedar of Loghur, with this further modification in their behalf, that they received the rice at the Company’s home price of 8 and 10 Rupees per bag.”

> _[Margin: Colonel Close’s Report on Mr. Henshaw’s suggestions for the relief of Poona.]_
> _[Margin: Further letter from Mr. Henshaw (19th June) urging the sale of part of the Company’s rice for the relief of Poona.]_
> _[Margin: Large additional quantity of rice expected.]_
> _[Margin: Permitted to sell as far as 30,000 bags.]_
> _[Margin: Thanks of the Paishwa for the assistance to Poona.]_

28\. Under date the 12th of June 1804 a letter was addressed by the Resident at Poona to the Secretary to Government, stating (in answer to the signification to him of the 8th of that month) that the Port of Bombay being again open for the export of grain must, no doubt, prove advantageous to the inhabitants at Poona, to promote which he did not conceive that it was possible for the Bombay Presidency to do more for the assistance of His Highness’ subjects than to allow the export of grain from this Port with as little restriction and at as low rates as possible; which having been referred to Mr. Henshaw, he thereon appears to have addressed on the 19th of June a separate letter to me (but which I placed at the time on the public record), suggesting, on the ground of this further representation from the Colonel, that the expediency of opening the Company’s Stores, and selling for exportation at a moderate price, appeared to be thereby so forcibly corroborated that he could not avoid applying again for authority to sell generally of Moonghy rice at 10 Rupees and Patcherry at 12, having attention never to bring our store (which was now overflowing) under 50,000 bags, at the same time that by the 15th of next month upwards of a lac of bags were, in addition, to be expected, adding that [p. 208] that the Paishwa's Carkoon was impatient for an answer as to the grain he wished to send off to Poona; upon all which the Secretary to Government was, under the same date, directed to signify that the accommodation contained in Mr. Henshaw's letter was acquiesced in, but that he was to make a further report when he had sold of 30,000 bags; which having been communicated to the Resident at Poona, he replied, on the 24th of June, in terms expressive of "the deep sense of His Highness the Paishwa of the constant and cordial disposition of Government to meet his wishes and promote the interest of the State."

> _[Margin: Resident at Poona's representation in favour of Byajee Naik being allowed to export grain to that place.]_
> _[Margin: Board's surprise expressed at there remaining so much difficulty in respect to the exportation of grain.]_
> _[Margin: Mr. Henshaw's report in reply.]_
> _[Margin: Board's order thereon.]_
> _[Margin: Case of the Soubahdar of Bassein applying to export grain he had purchased, and Mr. Henshaw's objections thereto, as militating against the Regulations.]_

29\. Under date the 2nd of June a letter was addressed by the Resident at Poona to the Secretary to Government, stating that permission having being granted to the Paishwa's servant Byajee Naik to export a few thousand bullock loads of grain, he had represented that a proportion of the stated quantity, amounting to 500 loads, still remained at Bombay, and had earnestly solicited the Resident to request that his agent might be allowed to forward it to Panwell, a copy of which letter was, on the 23rd of June, referred by the Secretary to Government to Mr. Henshaw, expressing to him, at the same time "the surprise of Government why the export of grain should, under the existing orders, thus appear to continue a matter of such favour and difficulty," to which Mr. Henshaw replied on the same day, stating that under the 5th of April, the abovenamed Byajee brought him a memorandum from Colonel Close, reciting that with the permission of the Honourable the Governor, the grain mentioned in the memorandum annexed was permitted to be exported to Panwell; the memorandum specified 2,500 bullock loads, which quantity Mr. Henshaw showed that he had already exported, as sanctioned by the countersignature of Colonel Close, upon which Mr. Henshaw further remarks—"I trust your letter is not meant as a censure in any part of my conduct in this transaction: it took place in April, and the same orders then existed as are now in force; yet you must be sensible, Sir, that favour alone could have procured to Byajee Naik that induldence as he expected in consequence of Government's commands, without adhering to any existing orders regulating this report." On consideration of which answer, the Secretary to Government was directed to signify to Mr. Henshaw on the 25th of June, that under the present circumstances of the abundance of the market, Byajee Naik (a confidential servant of His Highness [p. 209] the Paishwa) should be allowed to purchase and export 500 loads more than he had yet done, provided the same be purchased from the one-fourth of the cargoes which individuals might sell for that purpose; at the same time that it was but justice to him to notice that his (the Custom` Master's) official conduct appeared to be unobjectionable in the present instance, the grounds of the surprise of Government being that, whilst the one-fourth of rice imported was allowed to be exported, and that the Company was also selling for exportation, the Natives should still see occasion to resort to Government for their special sanction in respect to the export of this article, of which another instance appears by the records of the Country Correspondence Office to have about the same time occurred in consequence of a letter to me from the Soubahdar (or his Deputy) of Bassein, setting forth that 600 bags of Bengal rice had been purchased through Mahomed Hussein Cauzee, for the use of his (the Soubahdar's) people, for which he was about to send boats, when he understood “ that a stop had been put to the exportation of grain ;” wherefore the Soubahdar requested “ that from motives of friendship, I would permit this quantity to be exported, as they had already been purchased,” which from a similar motive of accommodation to these parties, has swayed the Board, two days thereafter on the occasion of the supply to Byajee Naik, viz. the comparative abundance of the market, and the Soubahdar's near connection with His Highness the Paishwa, I desired accordingly Mr. Bouchier, the then Acting Secretary and Translator in the office of Country Correspondence, to write, on the 23rd of June, to the Custom Master, to allow to be exported; upon which Mr. Henshaw applied to the Secretary, Mr. Grant, to know whether the above order (which Mr. Bouchier appears to have erroneously worded in the name of the Governor in Council) implied, without any reference to existing Regulations as to exports, or that they were to be “attended to :” whereupon Mr. Bouchier requested (as he has noted, to be by my desire) to know “how the Custom Master understood this to militate against existing Regulations ?” To which Mr. Henshaw replied, “because it forms no part of the one-fourth allowed to be exported ;” so that I know not whether the Soubahdar got this quantity or not, having, in fact, learnt thus much not from our records, but from the correspondence in Mr. Cumberlege's custody, as obtained by him of Mr. Henshaw. With a view, however, to obviate the recurrence of these repeated appeals to Government, Mr. Henshaw was, on the 25th of June, instructed to communicate to the Superintendent of Police the Regulation in force, by which persons might unobjectionably purchase and export rice and other grain, to the end that Mr. Halliday might cause the same to be [p. 210] proclaimed by beat of battakee; but when on the occasion of drawing up this narrative I called on Mr. Halliday for a statement of the terms in which that proclamation might have been made, he reported that none took place, and that Mr. Henshaw never communicated to him the Government order.

> _[Margin: Allusion to a suggestion for throwing the trade in rice entirely open.]_

30\. Under several dates in June and July I had some correspondence with Colonel Close on an intimation from the Honorable General Wellesley that the Colonel's opinion was in favour of throwing open altogether the trade in grain to the Continent, against which I sent the Colonel some reflections, furnished by Mr. Henshaw, in view to which, or to the other motives adverted to in the Colonel's answer, as per separate extracts in the series No. 2, he does not seem to have persevered in the idea of the practical eligibility of the measure; but the discussion led to a further reduction in the Company's selling prices for exportation, as will appear in the next paragraph, and to the establishing of a selling station for rice at Panwell, as well as to the charity at Poona, under the original suggestion, and promoted chiefly by Lady Mackintosh.

> _[Margin: Further reduction in the price of rice for exportation.]_

31\. On the 12th July 1804 Mr. Henshaw submitted in a letter to the Secretary to Government (as adverted to in the preceding paragraph) the expediency of reducing the export price of rice, in view to the more extensive relief of the poor of the Maratha countries, to 9½ Rupees per bag of Moonghy, and to 11 Rupees of Patcherry, which was agreed to on the 13th July, and the Superintendent of Police advised, with orders to proclaim the reduced export prices by beat of battakee.

> _[Margin: A lac of bags of rice exported in one month between July and August 1804, and intimation thereon to Mr. Henshaw to stop further sales for exportation, till the Board should decide in view to the stock on hand, which restriction was the next day withdrawn, and a full confidence expressed in his discretion as to the point in question.]_

32\. On the 25th of August Mr. Henshaw noticed to me that the call for rice exceeded any thing he could have supposed; for that since the 27th of July a full lac of bags had been sold for export, adding that "in ten days if the export was not under check not a bag would be left in the place," in view to which I wrote to him on the day following that "I thought it proper he should not dispose of any more rice for exportation till he should lay his statement of the stock on hand (which he had reported to be then reduced to little more than 80,000 bags) and expectancies before Government, as he was accordingly to do with the least delay, and to obtain their orders."


<!-- page: 223 | folio: 211 -->
33\. On further reflection, however, and lest Mr. Henshaw's refusing intermediately the offers of purchasers might produce bad consequences, I preferred to leave that matter to Mr. Henshaw's discretion, in which (as signified to him in a note of the 27th of August) "I felt persuaded that every confidence might be placed." In reply, Mr. Henshaw wrote to me on the same date—"I am much flattered by your confidence, which you may rest assured shall not be ill applied by any exercise of my discretion as to sales for export."

> _[Margin: Signification to Mr. Henshaw that the Board alone could authorise the alteration of the bazaar prices.]_

34\. Mr. Henshaw having (as I conclude from my answer) made in the beginning of September some verbal proposition respecting our bazaar prices, I wrote to him on the 5th of that month (as entered in the sequel of the series of the Correspondence No. 1) that "I was very doubtful whether anything should induce us to heighten them, but that, according to the Regulations, nothing could be done in respect to altering either, excepting upon a public report and official suggestion from him, such as the Board at large might exercise their judgment on," which I, therefore, advised him to make.

> _[Margin: Indication from Surat of undue fees being taken for permission to export grain.]_

35\. Under date the 6th of September the Magistrate at Surat advised me (as per Correspondence in the voucher No. 3) of the desire of some of the merchants at Surat to have permission to export from

Bombay such quantities of Bengal rice as might alleviate in that quarter the distress of the season, provided they could do so free of any customs or fees. Upon the receipt of the intimation, I directed Mr. Shank, my Private Secretary, to write to know whether there were fees of any description (not having then immediately in recollection whether the Custom House Regulations did not authorise some) on the exportation of grain; to which Mr.

> _[Margin: Mr. Henshaw's denial thereof.]_

Henshaw answered, on the 22nd of September, that "no fee of any description was collected on grain of any sort *exported*, either to Surat or any other port," which original denial on his part I immediately sent to Mr. Crow, who, in consequence, transmitted a letter from Dadabhoy Nasserwanjee Moody at Bombay to Pestonjee Calla at Surat, dated the 25th of August 1804, reciting that "when the Sircar allows exportation it takes one and a half or two Rupees on each bag for the permission, and order is given to pass it out."

36\. On my sending for the writer of the original letter in question, he urged having written merely from general impressions or hearsay, without possessing any real knowledge on the subject; on which occasion I, however, expressed my doubts to Mr. Crow whether in that respect this Native told the truth, and I, therefore, invited that gentleman to transmit such other [p. 212] documents as might be procurable on so interesting a subject. Mr. Crow accordingly transmitted another extract of a letter from Luckmichund Poonjraz at Bombay to Jadowjee Luckmichund at Surat, dated the 29th of August 1804, giving merely a price current of grain, with a notice at the bottom in these words—"in which appears will be an addition," which might refer to a probable general rise in the price; meanwhile Dadabhoy Nasserwanjee, the author of the first mentioned letter, delivered in a translate of it into English as made by himself, purporting, according to his annexed declaration (No. 4), that the 1½ or 2 Rupees therein mentioned was merely the additional price on exportation, or what persons entitled from their imports to pass notes, and not wanting them from disposing of all their cargo in town, would sell them for to those who desired to export. Upon this explanation, although not perhaps entirely satisfactory, the matter rested till after the recent trial of Abajee Bhow in the King's Court, but since then I have had a translation of it made in the Country Correspondence office, which implies that, independent of the price, what is herein denominated "the expense thereof" is from one and a half to two Rupees per bag, so that, on the whole, these inuendos, rather than exact information, may admit of further elucidation.

> _[Margin: Charge by Munoher Rowjee against Abajee Gunnesh, a Purvoe in the Grain Department.]_

37\. On the 21st September 1804 it was represented to me that Abajee Gunnesh Bhow, employed by Mr. Henshaw in in the Grain Department, had received from Munoher Rowjee to the amount of 17,000 Rupees and upwards, in money and goods, for procuring chits to pass rice and grain for exportation; and further, that the said Munoherdass had paid a fine of Rupees 950 for a quantity of 1,900 bags of grain, which had been detected in the attempt to be improperly passed, on which occasion Munoher stated to have also paid the sum of Rupees 4,000 to the aforesaid Bhow, for which he obtained the latter's receipt.

> _[Margin: Reference thereon to the Superintendent of Police.]_

38\. Upon this information I thought it my duty, in the recess of Council, to direct my Private Secretary to order. Mr. Henshaw to suspend the accused Purvoe; at the same time that the Superintendent of Police was directed to examine upon oath Munoherdass, in the said Purvoe's presence, on the foundation there might be for the above representation of circumstances, allowing Gunnesh to cross-question Munoherdass, and making thereon his report.

> _[Margin: Mr. Henshaw's remonstrances against and suggestions in respect to this order.]_

39\. In acknowledging the above order to him, Mr. Henshaw, by his letter of the 22nd of September 1804, professed obedience to it, but not until the Purvoe should deliver in his accounts; expressing disappointment that the matter was not referred to him in the first instance, [p. 213] when he would have requested some other gentleman to be joined with him in the investigation, preferring, however, that the matter should be left to its due course at law. On the same occasion Mr. Henshaw added that ‘‘ he felt a shameful stigma in the distrust (too evidently marked) of his energy to discover any misconduct of a person employed by him;’’ observing that although the party accused in this instance may avoid detection, still the disgrace of the charge against him must attach to the office in some degree, and bring a portion of delinquency with it. ‘‘I feel’’ (continues Mr. Henshaw) ‘‘ most sensibly, therefore, the shame of such a charge.’’

> _[Margin: Mr. Henshaw joined with Mr. Halliday in this investigation.]_
> _[Margin: Accuser refused to plead before this committee.]_

40\. Upon this remonstrance from Mr. Henshaw, he was, on the 23rd September 1804, appointed to act jointly with Mr. Halliday in the investigation of this charge; but the object was frustrated by Munoherdass the accuser’s refusing to say anything before them on the subject of his charge, further than that he had declared all he knew to his attorneys, Messieurs Thriepland and Cumberlege, without whose presence he would say nothing.

> _[Margin: Munoherdass’ own account of the particulars of the charge.]_

41\. On the day following Munoherdass addressed a letter to Government stating that, having previously consulted the professional gentlemen above named on the means of recovering the large sums of money advanced by him to Bhow, he had, by their advice, intended to seek redress in the Court of the Recorder, and to state his grievances to Messrs. Henshaw and Halliday in such general terms as might not be taken advantage of in the intended legal process, but that he was yet ready to make a disclosure to Government not incompatible with his own interests, for that the payments, as he had already stated them, were true, and matter of fact, as he had that day made affidavit of, viz., that Abajee Gunnesh, *alias* Bhow, was justly and truly indebted to this deponent in the sum of twenty thousand Rupees and upwards, for so much by him had and received for this deponent’s use, as per the following particulars:—


| Col 1 | Col 2 | Col 3 | Col 4 | Col 5 | Col 6 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 3rd March 1804, so much had and received by defendant |  |  | .. | Rs. | 4,000 |
| 5th April | ditto | ditto | ditto | ’’ | 3,400 |
| 7th April | ditto | ditto | ditto | ’’ | 3,950 |
| 26th April | ditto | ditto | ditto | ’’ | 2,000 |
| 8th May | ditto | ditto | ditto | ’’ | 1,650 |
| 12th May | Hemp | ditto | ditto | ’’ | 800 |
| 12th May | Cash | ditto | ditto | ’’ | 1,600 |
| 14th May | ditto | ditto | ditto | ’’ | 4,440 |
| Rupees |  |  |  |  | 21,840 |


<!-- page: 226 | folio: 214 -->
> _[Margin: Company's Counsel's opinion in that stage of the cause.]_

42 Upon this information it was ordered by the Board, on the 29th of September 1804, that a copy of all the papers respecting it should be referred to the Company's Standing Counsel for his opinion and report on the means to be pursued by Government for prosecuting Abbajee Gunnesh Bhow, as a person charged with corruption in office whilst employed in the service of Mr. Henshaw in the Grain Department of Government, in pursuance of which Mr. Thriepland reported, on the 5th of October, that Munoherdass Rowjee, having consulted him professionally before the receipt of the Board's reference on the subject of the fraud practised on him by Abajee aforesaid, it had thereon become his (Mr. Thriepland's) duty to advise the commencement of a Common Law action, which would be accordingly tried in the course of the ensuing term, the event of which he counselled Government to abide previous to attempting the defendant's conviction criminally.

> _[Margin: Abajee removed from the Grain Department.]_

43\. Upon the receipt of this opinion, Mr. Henshaw was called upon, under date 7th of October 1804, to state from what period he could dispense with the services of Abajee Gunnesh Bhow, as being a man against whom such acts of corruption are charged upon affidavit as to render it highly indecorous and unfit that he should longer have access to the Company's offices or accounts, or hold any trust, ostensible or otherwise, in their concerns, until at least his conduct be cleared, by the issue of the suit now proceeding with, from the imputations that so prominently stand against it; in reply to which Mr. Henshaw reported, on the 8th of October, that, "as no choice of measures was left to him, he should accordingly dismiss the said Bhow, in the firm confidence that he should not find the trust he had hitherto reposed in him in any way abused or misplaced."

> _[Margin: Process against Abajee changed from a civil to a criminal suit, and information furnished thereon from the Custom House.]_

44\. It has since appeared that Mr. Thriepland, acting in his private capacity as Counsel for Munoherdass, had found occasion to alter the mode of legal process, from a civil suit to a criminal prosecution, as was announced in a letter from him of the 5th of March 1805, written as Company's Counsel to Mr. Cumberlege, in that gentleman's capacity of Solicitor for Crown Prosecutions, specifying that the Grand Jury having found two Bills against Abajee Gunnesh, the one for extortion at Common Law, and the other under the Statute 33 of the King, Mr. Thriepland thereupon desired the said Mr. Cumberlege to apply to Government to have certain books of the Custom House delivered up, to enable him to substantiate the charge before the Jury, as was accordingly ordered in the 8th of that month and year; and upon Mr. Thriepland's subsequent [p. 215] application of the 26th of March 1805 Mr. Wappers was, on the 28th, directed to furnish Mr. Thriepland with such further information as his long experience in the Custom House Department might enable him to afford.

> _[Margin: Conviction of Abajee Gunnesh.]_
> _[Margin: Notice thereon to Mr. Henshaw.]_

45\. On the 17th of April 1805 Mr. Cumberlege reported, in his capacity of Solicitor for Crown Prosecutions, that Abajee Gunnesh had on the day before been found guilty, after a trial of 14 hours, of having received Rupees 21,840 from Munoherdass Rowjee for promising pass notes from the Custom Master for the exportation of grain to the adjoining continent, to the extent of 3,000 bags of rice and 900 candies of bajree, joaree, and black gram, during the months of March, April, and May 1804, in defiance of the existing regulations of Government; adding that the Honorable Company's Standing Counsel, who conducted the prosecution, meant to forward a circumstantial statement of the whole to Government, of all which Mr. Henshaw was advised on the 23rd of April, with information that Government reserved any further notice that might appear to be required on their part till the expected report in detail from Mr. Thriepland.

> _[Margin: Mr. Thriepland's report of this trial.]_
> _[Margin: Insufficient state of the Cash Book received from the Custom House.]_
> _[Margin: And as to the pass notes.]_

46\. This detailed report from Mr. Thriepland was accordingly received under date the 10th, and stands recorded in our Proceedings of the 15th of May 1805, wherein, after adverting to the great unwillingness with which the requisite information was furnished to him at the Custom House, Mr. Thriepland proceeds more particularly to animadvert on the insufficient state in which he received the copy of the Grain Cash Book for March 1804 (to which period the charge was known to refer); as exhibiting merely a collection of numerals both on the debit and credit side of the account, and containing scarcely any names or references, though the book from which it was taken was full of both, whilst the mass of pass notes were delivered to him in such a state of confusion as required much labour to extricate them from.

> _[Margin: Circumstances on which this prosecution is founded.]_
> _[Margin: Instance of 4 pass notes containing mis-statements of fact in the body of them, for the purpose of enabling Munoherdass to export more grain than he was entitled to, as if purchased from the Company.]_

47\. This report next adverts to Mr. Henshaw's employ of Abajee Gunnesh notwithstanding his being warned of his bad character, followed by the mention that among those who dealt largely with the Company for the grain we had ourselves collected in view to the scarcity was one Bugwan Culloo, whose business was principally carried on by his nephew Munoherdass, who likewise traded, sometimes on his own account [p. 216] and occasionally in partnership with one Munoher Maljee. Between the 4th of January and 3rd of April 1804 Munoherdass had, either on his own or his uncle's account, purchased from the Company's store to the value of 1,52,727 Rupees, 2 quarters, 18 reas, all which he again exported, and paid money besides to the prisoner Abajee Gunnesh as the price of passes to export grain, which the prisoner falsely certified had been purchased from the Company, in proof of which Mr. Thriepland found, on examining the mass of pass notes, four bearing date in April 1804 with the prisoner's initials affixed to each, and countersigned by the Custom Master, authorising the export of no less a quantity than 2,200 bags of rice, amounting in value to Rs. 26,400, on the ground of the same having been purchased from the Company.

> _[Margin: Distinction between the regular and irregular pass notes.]_

48\. Besides this, it was next shown by Mr. Thriepland that there were two descriptions of pass notes kept in the Grain Department of the Custom House : the one regular, and purporting to be granted after due examination and report, ascertaining in the case of purchasers from individuals the title of the party to export under the regulation of Government; the other being without that regular reference and report, or certificate, that the grain had been manifested, to justify the proposed export, of which last class of applications, converted by Mr. Henshaw's initials into irregular pass notes, Mr. Thriepland produced twenty-two in the name of Bhugwan Culloo, or of Munoher Rowjee, which clandestine pass notes the export Purvoe refused to pass, until so commanded either from Abajee or the Custom Master.

> _[Margin: Supposed extensive exports of grain in the same clandestine manner.]_

49\. It is on this occasion further surmised by Mr. Thriepland, on the probable grounds adverted to in his report, that much more grain, the property of private importers, must have been allowed to be thus objectionably passed out than has been brought to light in the course of this prosecution.

> _[Margin: Particulars as to the consideration paid for exempting the smuggled grain in February 1804 from confiscation.]_

50\. In the subsequent part of his report Mr. Thriepland explains that the first 4,000 Rs. charged by the prosecutor against Gunnesh Bhow (as per particulars in the preceding 39th paragraph) was by way of consideration to Abajee Gunnesh for his interest with Mr. Henshaw in preventing the confiscation of a quantity of grain embarked on boats in February 1804 without any pass whatever from the Custom House (as stands already noticed in the 16th paragraph of this [p. 217] narrative), for which sum the said Munoher gave the defendant a writing, in the name of his uncle, Bugwan Culloo, as for so much money received on loan under date the 3rd March 1804; and it appears by Mr. Henshaw's official report on that occasion that the smuggling attempt in question was first detected in the latter end of February, and that on the 15th of March 1804 he wrote in to the Board to dispense with the confiscation, and be satisfied with a fine, which does not, however, appear to have been credited in the Fine Book of the Custom House till August following.

51\. Mr. Thriepland next refers to a petition presented to me on the part of Abajee Gunnesh previous to the January Sessions, wherein that person professing no small contempt for his prosecutor, Mr. Thriepland, takes occasion thence to argue the unlikelihood of the defendant's plea as to the four thousand Rupees in question being a *bonâ fide* loan to his accuser.

> _[Margin: Abajee's representation as to his own merits in the Grain Department.]_
> _[Margin: In borrowing money on his own credit to carry on the concern.]_

52\. In the letter to me or petition from Abajee Gunnesh, which has been above referred to by Mr. Thriepland, that person takes credit to himself for having raised money by borrowing on his private responsibility to enable Mr. Henshaw to discharge the earlier demands of the grain merchants, because Mr. Henshaw declined to apply for any advance of cash from the Company's Treasury, giving as a reason that the funds in it would not then conveniently admit thereof, respecting which what passed in correspondence between me and Mr. Henshaw will be found in the 5th preceding paragraph.

> _[Margin: As 2ndly, to the great profit by his good management to the Company.]_
> _[Margin: How Abajee was assured by Mr. Henshaw that he would be finally rewarded.]_

53\. Abajee further states that Mr. Henshaw required of him to conduct his duties in the Grain Department with care and upon honour, which he accordingly did, to an aggregate amounting, from January to April 1804, of 11,55,500 Rs., yielding a net profit to the Honorable Company, clear of all charges, of two lacs ten thousand Rupees, as would appear, he said, by the books balanced up to the end of the year of account, whilst from May to September there would, he observed, prove a profit of three or four lacs more, besides supplying, on an exigency referred to, the Company's Treasury in advance, as set forth at length in his accompanying petition No. 6, which soon after its receipt I made over, with its accompanying note from Mr. Travers, to Mr. Cumberlege; but it is no more than fair to the defendant that it should also be thus far noticed on our records, and that its more prominent assertions should find some place in the present narrative, among which one is that "having been confined by sickness to my bed, by extraordinary exertions in the discharge of my duties, I reported it to Mr. Henshaw, entreating my resignation of the [p. 218] services imposed on me in the Grain Department, not only for that reason, but from the ill-will and enmity I subjected myself to, on account of this grain business, from those whose wishes were not gratified in that respect; but the Custom Master, regardless of those representations, assured me that it was sufficient for us to be attentive to the interests of our employers, and he further assured me that he would recommend me to Government for a handsome reward for the pains and labour bestowed by me in this Grain Department, to be given at the conclusion of this office."

> _[Margin: Cause assigned for the merchants' ill-will against him.]_

54\. This document will be found further to state that, between the 22nd and 23rd of August, Mr. Henshaw ordered him to dispose of about twenty thousand bags of rice at the limited price of Rupees 9-2-0 per bag for exportation, on condition that I allowed thereof, or otherwise the bargain to be deemed null and void, at which period the quantity of rice on hand did not, he says, exceed sixty thousand bags; and there being no prospect of rain, Mr. Henshaw reported to me (as the defendant sets forth) the balance on hand by a private note, upon which I was pleased, he adds, on the 24th of the same month, to direct the Custom Master not to dispose of a single bag of rice, which very much disappointed the conditional purchasers of the twenty thousand bags, and rendered them all inimical to him, as within those two days the prices had risen from nine and a half to eleven and a half, whence they conceived themselves losers of two Rupees on every bag. On this part of Abajee's representation my correspondence with Mr. Henshaw of the 25th, 26th, and 27th of August, will be found to throw a consi_ derable light, and to which I accordingly refer, as already adverted to in the 32nd preceding paragraph, and in the correspondence at length in the voucher No. 1.

> _[Margin: His reply to the particular charge against him.]_

55\. The defendant next proceeds to lament the impeachment of his upright character by what he describes "a groundless and fabricated charge from a common servant of a Banian, who has (he adds) been also discharged from his employment for actual bad behaviour of this nature." He then infers the improbability of his having received, as pretended by his accuser, a considerable present on account of passports, and fines from the export office, being entirely separate and distinct from his own Grain Gepartment, the Head Clerk in the former being a Portuguese acting under fixed rules sanctioned by the Custom Master for his guidance; observing that every pass note for the export articles is generally countersigned by the Custom Master, and then the notes go to the officer of the export department, who gives a passport, under his signature, to the gatekeeper; he only certified the pass notes for such grain as was declared to have been purchased from the stock of the Honorable Company; and such notes, after being also countersigned by [p. 219] the Custom Master, were sent to the officer of the export department, who duly registered and gave passes to the purchasers respectively to export their grain. He also equally denies having benefited by the intercepted grain, and states finally that he was employed in no other than the grain department to receive money for such grain as was sold and to keep an account thereof.

> _[Margin: Mr. Henshaw called upon to answer in respect to Mr. Thriepland's report.]_

56\. On the date of Mr. Thriepland's report being recorded, a copy of it was transmitted to Mr. Henshaw for his information, and such reply as might enable Government to form a judgment, particularly as far as respected the insufficient copy of the grain cash book; secondly, the confused state of the pass notes; and, thirdly, to account for the irregularity of the two and twenty pass notes, and, moreover, to explain the system he seems to have observed in respect to the sale of grain on credit, and whether any interest was received thereon; and finally to account for the altering and tearing of the leaves in the book, or books, of record in his department.

> _[Margin: Accountant General called on to prepare statement of the imports and exports of grain.]_

57\. In reference also to the suspicion entertained by Mr. Thriepland of more grain having passed clandestinely than has yet been ascertained, the Accountant General was, on the same day, called upon to lay before the Board a figured statement of all the grain imported since the first period of the restrictions on its export in September 1803 to that of the proclamation for a free export from the 21st of April 1805, contrasting therewith the amount of the Company's sales for exportation and the prescribed proportion which the merchant importers were allowed to export, exclusive of the small discretional quantities that the Custom Master, from necessity, admitted of to boats and vessels for the provision of their crews, which, together with the quantity consumed on the Island, or sent under the authority of Government to Salsette, Poona, Surat, or elsewhere, ought to make up the whole export and general consumption, exhibiting thereon the stock on hand, respecting which if there should prove any difference, the Accountant General was to call on the Custom Master, and, after receiving from him the requisite explanations, to make his report.

> _[Margin: Narrain Kemjee suspended from office.]_
> _[Margin: Approbation signified to Mr. Thriepland.]_

58\. It appearing, in the course of Mr Thriepland's remarks, that Narrain Kemjee, an officer in the Custom House, stood charged on oath with having received a bribe of five hundred Rupees, he was on the same date ordered to stand suspended from his office till he should have been cleared of this charge, of all which Mr. Thriepland was advised, accompanied by an intimation of the Board's opinion that he had great merit in the pains he had taken to expose the corrupt system on which he had thus reported.


<!-- page: 232 | folio: 220 -->
> _[Margin: Mr. Henshaw's letter in reply to Mr. Thriepland's report.]_
> _[Margin: As to the Cash Book and Pass Notes.]_

59\. On the 9th of June Mr. Henshaw delivered in his answer, dated the day preceding, expressing equal surprise and concern to perceive his conduct had been construed into an indisposition to let the charge against Abajee Gunnesh have all the necessary elucidation, as in the instances of the insufficient copy of the grain cash book and the confused state of the pass notes; observing, as to the first article, that the copy of the account in question is a faithful transcript from the original one, and that had he delivered any other he must have been blamed; and with respect to the pass notes they are, he remarks, vouchers necessary only for twenty-four hours, adding that they were formerly burnt once a month; and had in the present instance been preserved in the desk of a Purvoe, whence they were delivered, on being called for by Mr. Thriepland, in the state they were found.

60\. With respect to the irregular passes, Mr. Henshaw refers to the 4th Clause of Section 8th, Regulation VI. 1799, stating that grain of all sorts is to be exempted from duty, and the *Clerk of the Market* is to send a weekly report to the Custom Master of the prices of the different articles of grain, that in the event of any uncommon decrease in the quantity at market a temporary prohibition may be laid on the exportation of it; under which authority, joined to the flattering terms in which the Governor in Council had been pleased to notice his exertions, was he (Mr. Henshaw asks) to consider those flattering terms as indicative of any restriction of that authority as Custom Master which he before possessed? Was he (adds he) to consider the many and repeated instances of his possessing the Honorable the Governor's unlimited confidence, as expressed to him both by note and personally, as indicative of any curtailment of his former authority? Did not (continues Mr. Henshaw) these circumstances, on the contrary, fully confirm to me in the grain department all that discretionary authority already vested in me as Custom Master by the 4th Clause of Section VIII. of the Regulations above quoted?

> _[Margin: Mr. Henshaw's avowal of having granted the irregular pass notes in reward for Abajee's services in the Grain Department.]_

61\.
